Writing \(M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor\), \(n_p=\lfloor(n-M_0)/2\rfloor\), \(n_f=n-M_0-n_p\), \(m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor\), \(m_f=m-m_p\), \(M_p=n_p+m_p\), \(M_f=n_f+m_f\), \(u=M_0/8\), \(t_p=M_p/8\), and \(t=M_f/8\), the Poisson hybrid risk is \[\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon) = \begin{cases} \displaystyle \int \left\{ Z^{\mathrm{mix}}(K)-\tau(P) \right\}^2 \,d\mathbb P^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{P,u,t_p,t}(K), & \mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m),\\[1.2em] \displaystyle \tau(P)^2, & \text{otherwise,} \end{cases}\] where \(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}\) is the calibrated hybrid statistic of Definition 25.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 14.

Take \(C=384\). Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), \(d\ge2\), and \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). The estimator is measurable because it is either the constant zero map or, on the calibrated branch of Algorithm 1, a finite linear combination of measurable prefix-count statistics. It remains to prove the displayed risk bound.

1. Suppose first that \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds. Put \[M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad M_p=n_p+m_p,\qquad M_f=n_f+m_f,\] and \[u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad t_p=\frac{M_p}{8},\qquad t=\frac{M_f}{8}.\] Let \(\mathbb P_3\) be the three-pool product law whose complete-record coordinate has law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\), while the two shared coordinates have law \(P_{XA}\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}\). Let \(\boldsymbol\lambda=(u,t_p,t)\) and let the capacity vector be \(\boldsymbol M=(M_0,M_p,M_f)\). For a three-prefix array \[\mathfrak s=(\mathfrak s_0,\mathfrak s_p,\mathfrak s_f),\] define \[\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s) = Z^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,\epsilon,u,t} \bigl(\operatorname{hist}(\mathfrak s_0), \operatorname{hist}(\mathfrak s_p), \operatorname{hist}(\mathfrak s_f)\bigr),\] where the first histogram records complete outcome-labeled observations and the last two record treatment-covariate observations. This prefix statistic is measurable and satisfies \[-1\le \Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)\le 1 \quad\text{for every }\mathfrak s, \qquad -1\le \tau(P)\le 1,\] the first bound coming from clipping in Algorithm 1 and the second from Definition 8 together with occupied-cell overlap in Definition 6.

2. For fixed pools \[\mathfrak p=(\mathfrak p_0,\mathfrak p_p,\mathfrak p_f) \in \bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\bigr)^{M_0} \times \bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}\bigr)^{M_p} \times \bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}\bigr)^{M_f},\] define the Rao–Blackwellized prefix statistic by \[\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p) = \sum_{r_0,r_p,r_f\ge0} \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)=r_0\} \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t_p)=r_p\} \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)=r_f\} \,\Psi^\circ(\mathfrak p;r_0,r_p,r_f),\] where \[\Psi^\circ(\mathfrak p;r_0,r_p,r_f) = \begin{cases} \Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon} \bigl(\mathfrak p_0[1{:}r_0], \mathfrak p_p[1{:}r_p], \mathfrak p_f[1{:}r_f]\bigr), & r_0\le M_0,\ r_p\le M_p,\ r_f\le M_f,\\ 0,&\text{otherwise.} \end{cases}\] Under \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\), expanding the finite sums in Algorithm 1 gives the pointwise identity \[\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m) = \operatorname{RB}\bigl(\mathfrak P(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)\bigr),\] where \(\mathfrak P(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)\) is the deterministic three-pool split formed from the first \(M_0\) complete labeled records, the next \(n_p\) labeled records projected to \((X,A)\) together with the first \(m_p\) auxiliary records, and the remaining \(n_f\) projected labeled records together with the remaining \(m_f\) auxiliary records. By Definition 9, this split has law equal to the fixed-pool law with coordinate law \(\mathbb P_3\) and capacities \(\boldsymbol M\). Hence \[\mathbb E\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] = \int \bigl\{\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2 \,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{fix}}(\mathfrak p).\]

3. The finite-pool-to-Poisson comparison used here is the following. For any prefix statistic with values in an interval \([a,b]\), any target and overflow value in \([a,b]\), and independent Poisson requested lengths with means \(\boldsymbol\lambda\), \[\int \bigl\{\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)-\theta\bigr\}^2 \,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{fix}}(\mathfrak p) \le \int \bigl\{\Psi(\mathfrak s)-\theta\bigr\}^2 \,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{Pois}}(\mathfrak s) + (b-a)^2 \sum_{\iota\in\{0,p,f\}} \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda_\iota)>M_\iota\}.\] Indeed, \(\operatorname{RB}\) is the conditional expectation of the capped randomized-prefix statistic given the fixed pools, so Jensen’s inequality bounds its squared risk by the capped randomized-prefix risk. Off the overflow event the capped and uncapped prefixes agree; on overflow both the capped value and the target lie in \([a,b]\), so the extra squared loss is bounded by \((b-a)^2\) times the overflow probability. Applying this with \(a=-1\), \(b=1\), \(\theta=\tau(P)\), overflow value \(0\), and \(\Psi=\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}\) gives \[\int \bigl\{\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2 \,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{fix}}(\mathfrak p) \le \int \bigl\{\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2 \,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{Pois}}(\mathfrak s) + 4\bigl[ \pi_0+\pi_p+\pi_f \bigr],\] where \[\pi_0=\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\},\qquad \pi_p=\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t_p)>M_p\},\qquad \pi_f=\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>M_f\}.\] The image of the independent Poisson-prefix law under the three histograms is exactly \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{P,u,t_p,t}\), and therefore \[\int \bigl\{\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2 \,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{Pois}}(\mathfrak s) = \mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)\] on the calibrated branch.

4. It remains to bound the three overflow probabilities. The calibration predicate gives \(24\le n\). From the definitions of the integer blocks, \[n\le4M_0,\qquad n\le4M_p,\qquad n\le4M_f,\] and all three capacities are positive. For any positive integer \(M_\bullet\) satisfying \(n\le4M_\bullet\), \[\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(M_\bullet/8)>M_\bullet\} \le \frac{8}{M_\bullet} \le \frac{32}{n}.\] To prove the first inequality, let \(X_\bullet\sim\operatorname{Poi}(M_\bullet/8)\). The overflow event is contained in the self-normalized Bernstein event at parameter \(M_\bullet/4\), because \[\sqrt{2\cdot\frac{M_\bullet}{8}\cdot\frac{M_\bullet}{4}} =\frac{M_\bullet}{4}\] and \(X_\bullet>M_\bullet\) implies \[X_\bullet-\frac{M_\bullet}{8} >\frac{7M_\bullet}{8} >\frac{M_\bullet}{4}+2\cdot\frac{M_\bullet}{4}.\] The Poisson Bernstein bound for this larger event gives \[\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(M_\bullet/8)>M_\bullet\} \le \exp(-M_\bullet/4) = \frac1{\exp(M_\bullet/4)} \le \frac{4}{M_\bullet} \le \frac{8}{M_\bullet},\] where \(1+M_\bullet/4\le \exp(M_\bullet/4)\) gives the penultimate inequality. Consequently \[\pi_0+\pi_p+\pi_f\le \frac{96}{n},\] and the preceding display yields \[\mathbb E\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le \mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon) +\frac{384}{n}.\] Since \(\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)\ge0\), this is at most \[2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon) +\frac{384}{n}.\]

5. If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) fails, then Algorithm 1 sets \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}=0\), while the definition of the hybrid Poisson risk gives \[\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)=\tau(P)^2.\] Because the annotation law is a probability measure, \[\mathbb E\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] = \tau(P)^2 \le 2\tau(P)^2+\frac{384}{n} = 2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)+\frac{384}{n},\] using \(n\ge1\). The two cases prove the asserted bound with the universal constant \(C=384\).

∎

Lemma 14 converts the Poisson risk calculation into the fixed-\(n,m\) annotation experiment of Definition 9. The additional \(C/n\) term is on the labeled-outcome scale already present in the rate, so combining Lemmas 13 and 14 yields the finite-sample upper-bound ingredient used in Theorem 2.

The final calibration statements make the Chebyshev constants explicit. The first controls the coefficient norm of the shifted quotient, and the second records the numerical bounds used in the finite calibration predicate of Algorithm 1.

Lemma 15 [lem:shifted-chebyshev-quotient-coefficient-bound] (Shifted quotient coefficient bound).

Let \(L\) be a positive integer, and let \(G_L\) be the polynomial continuation at zero of \((1-E_L(z))/z\) from Algorithm 1. For a real polynomial \(p(z)=\sum_i a_i z^i\), write \[\|p\|_1=\sum_i |a_i|\] for its coefficient \(\ell^1\)-norm. Then \[\|G_L\|_1\le 6^L .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 15.

Let \(L\ge 1\). We use the notation for \(H_L,E_L,G_L\) from Algorithm 1. The phrase “polynomial continuation at zero” is used through the quotient identities \[zE_L(z)=H_L(z),\qquad zG_L(z)=1-E_L(z),\] with equality in \(\mathbb R[z]\); the cancellations below use only that multiplication by the nonzero indeterminate \(z\) is injective on \(\mathbb R[z]\).

  1. Define, for every \(\alpha\in\mathbb N\), \[\theta_{L,\alpha} = (-1)^\alpha \frac{2^{2\alpha+3}}{L(L+\alpha+2)} \binom{L+\alpha+2}{2\alpha+4},\] and put \[\widetilde G_L(z) = \sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}\theta_{L,\alpha}z^\alpha .\] We first derive the shifted-Chebyshev coefficient expansion used below. Write \(Q_r(z)=T_r(1-2z)\). The initial polynomials and the defining Chebyshev recurrence give \[Q_0(z)=1,\qquad Q_1(z)=1-2z,\qquad Q_{r+2}(z)=2(1-2z)Q_{r+1}(z)-Q_r(z).\] For \(r\in\mathbb N\) and \(\nu\in\mathbb Z\), define \[\beta_{r,\nu}:= \begin{cases} 1, & \nu=0,\\[0.3em] (-1)^\nu\dfrac{r}{r+\nu} \binom{r+\nu}{2\nu}4^\nu, & 1\le\nu\le r,\\[0.8em] 0, & \nu<0\text{ or }r<\nu . \end{cases}\] Comparing coefficients in the displayed recurrence reduces the induction to \[\beta_{r+2,\nu} =2\beta_{r+1,\nu}-4\beta_{r+1,\nu-1}-\beta_{r,\nu}.\] For \(1\le\nu\le r\), write \[A_{r,\nu}:=\frac{r}{r+\nu}\binom{r+\nu}{2\nu}.\] Set \(A_{r,0}:=1\). For \(\nu\ge1\), the factorial form is \[A_{r,\nu} =\frac{r(r+\nu-1)!}{(2\nu)!(r-\nu)!},\] After cancelling the common factor \((-1)^\nu4^\nu\), the coefficient recurrence is \[A_{r+2,\nu} =2A_{r+1,\nu}+A_{r+1,\nu-1}-A_{r,\nu}.\] For \(1\le\nu\le r\), multiply this equality by \((2\nu)!(r+2-\nu)!/(r+\nu-1)!\). It becomes \[\begin{aligned} &(r+2)(r+\nu+1)(r+\nu)\\ &\quad = 2(r+1)(r+\nu)(r+2-\nu) +(r+1)(2\nu)(2\nu-1)\\ &\qquad\quad -r(r+2-\nu)(r+1-\nu), \end{aligned}\] and expanding both sides proves it. At \(\nu=0\), the term \(\beta_{r+1,-1}\) is zero by the outside-range convention, and the identity becomes \(1=2\cdot1-0-1\). At \(\nu=r+1\) and \(\nu=r+2\), the same zero convention leaves, respectively, \[\begin{aligned} (-1)^{r+1}(r+2)4^{r+1} &=2\left((-1)^{r+1}\frac{4^{r+1}}2\right) -4\left((-1)^r(r+1)4^r\right),\\ (-1)^{r+2}\frac{4^{r+2}}2 &=-4\left((-1)^{r+1}\frac{4^{r+1}}2\right), \end{aligned}\] so the recurrence holds there as well. Since the formula agrees with \(Q_0,Q_1\), induction proves, for every real \(z\), \[T_L(1-2z) = 1+ \sum_{1\le \nu\le L} (-1)^\nu\frac{L}{L+\nu} \binom{L+\nu}{2\nu}4^\nu z^\nu .\] The \(\nu=1\) term is \(-2L^2z\). For the remaining terms write \(\nu=\alpha+2\). Then \[(-1)^{\alpha+2}\frac{L}{L+\alpha+2} \binom{L+\alpha+2}{2\alpha+4}4^{\alpha+2}z^{\alpha+2} = 2L^2\theta_{L,\alpha}z^{\alpha+2},\] so, with \(Q_L(z)=T_L(1-2z)\), \[Q_L(z) = 1-2L^2z+2L^2z^2\widetilde G_L(z).\] Using these quotient identities, since \(Q_L(0)=1\), the polynomial \[H_L(z)=\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}\] has zero constant term. Moreover \(Q_L'(0)=-2T_L'(1)=-2L^2\), so \(H_L'(0)=1\), and hence the quotient identity for \(E_L\) gives \(E_L(0)=1\). Thus \(1-E_L\) has zero constant term, as required for the quotient identity defining \(G_L\). Using the displayed formula for \(Q_L\), \[H_L(z) =\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2} =z-z^2\widetilde G_L(z) =z(1-z\widetilde G_L(z)).\] Thus \[zE_L(z)=z(1-z\widetilde G_L(z)).\] Because multiplication by the nonzero indeterminate \(z\) is cancellative in \(\mathbb R[z]\), \[E_L(z)=1-z\widetilde G_L(z).\] Consequently \[zG_L(z)=1-E_L(z)=z\widetilde G_L(z),\] and the same cancellation gives \[G_L(z)=\widetilde G_L(z)\] as polynomials.

  2. Since \[\widetilde G_L(z)=\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}\theta_{L,\alpha}z^\alpha,\] every coefficient of \(\widetilde G_L\) outside the range \(0\le \alpha<L-1\) is zero. Hence \[\|G_L\|_1 = \|\widetilde G_L\|_1 = \sum_{\alpha\in\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde G_L)} |[z^\alpha]\widetilde G_L| \le \sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|.\]

  3. The last sum is the positive coefficient sum at \(1\): \[\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}| = \sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|\,1^\alpha .\] The coefficient formula also gives \[|\theta_{L,\alpha}| = \frac{2^{2\alpha+3}}{L(L+\alpha+2)} \binom{L+\alpha+2}{2\alpha+4},\] because \(L>0\) and all factors except \((-1)^\alpha\) are nonnegative.

  4. It remains to bound this positive coefficient sum. Write \[\Theta_L^+ = \sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|.\] This is the positive coefficient sum at the argument \(1\). The sign identity \((-1)^\alpha\theta_{L,\alpha}=|\theta_{L,\alpha}|\) gives \[\Theta_L^+ = \sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}\theta_{L,\alpha}(-1)^\alpha = \widetilde G_L(-1).\] Substituting \(z=-1\) in the identity \[Q_L(z)=1-2L^2z+2L^2z^2\widetilde G_L(z)\] and using \(Q_L(-1)=T_L(3)\) gives \[T_L(3)=1+2L^2+2L^2\Theta_L^+ .\] Since \(\Theta_L^+\ge0\) and \(L\ge1\), \[\Theta_L^+ \le 2L^2\Theta_L^+ \le 1+2L^2+2L^2\Theta_L^+ = T_L(3).\] It remains to bound \(T_L(3)\). Put \(a_\nu=T_\nu(3)\). The Chebyshev recurrence gives \[a_0=1,\qquad a_1=3,\qquad a_{\nu+2}=6a_{\nu+1}-a_\nu .\] Let \(\lambda_+=3+\sqrt8\) and \(\lambda_-=3-\sqrt8\). Since \(\lambda_++\lambda_-=6\) and \(\lambda_+\lambda_-=1\), both roots satisfy \(x^2=6x-1\). Consequently \[b_\nu:=\frac{\lambda_+^\nu+\lambda_-^\nu}{2}\] satisfies \(b_{\nu+2}=6b_{\nu+1}-b_\nu\), while \[b_0=1=a_0,\qquad b_1=\frac{\lambda_++\lambda_-}{2}=3=a_1.\] Induction in the common recurrence gives \(a_\nu=b_\nu\), so \[a_\nu = \frac{(3+\sqrt8)^\nu+(3-\sqrt8)^\nu}{2},\] and in particular \(a_\nu\ge0\), because \(2<\sqrt8<3\) makes both \(\lambda_+\) and \(\lambda_-\) positive. A two-step induction now gives \(a_\nu\le6^\nu\): the cases \(\nu=0,1\) are immediate, and if the bound holds for \(\nu\) and \(\nu+1\), then \[a_{\nu+2}=6a_{\nu+1}-a_\nu \le 6a_{\nu+1} \le 6\cdot 6^{\nu+1} =6^{\nu+2}.\] Thus \(T_L(3)=a_L\le6^L\), and therefore \[\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}| = \Theta_L^+ \le 6^L .\]

  5. Combining the preceding inequalities yields \[\|G_L\|_1 \le \sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}| \le 6^L,\] which proves the claim.

∎

Lemma 15 gives a direct coefficient bound for the quotient polynomial \(G_L\). This estimate is the coefficient-growth input behind the explicit factorial second-moment calibration.

Lemma 16 [lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration] (Explicit Chebyshev calibration).

Let \(T_j\) be the Chebyshev polynomial of degree \(j\), set \(Q_j(z)=T_j(1-2z)\), and let \(\|\cdot\|_1\) denote coefficient \(\ell^1\)-norm. For every integer \(L\ge2\), the polynomial continuations \(E_L\) and \(G_L\) satisfy the following calibration.

  • (Coefficient bounds.) \[\|Q_L\|_1\le 7^L, \qquad 2\|G_L\|_1\le 14^L .\]

  • (Factorial-lift second moment.) For every \(a\in\{0,1\}\), every \(t>0\), \(B>0\), and every \(s_0,s_1\ge0\) with \(L\le tB\), set \(p=s_0+s_1\). If \(K_b\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_b)\) independently and \(\widehat W_{a,L}\) denotes the factorial lift of the light-cell polynomial based on \(G_L\), then \[E\widehat W_{a,L}^{2} \le 7056^L\left(1+\frac{p}{B}\right)^{2L},\] and, whenever \(p\le B\), \[E\widehat W_{a,L}^{2}\le 7056^L .\]

  • (Interval identities.) For every \(z\in[0,1]\), \[0\le E_L(z), \qquad E_L(z)\le 1, \qquad zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1 .\] Moreover, for every \(z\in(0,1]\), \[E_L(z)\le \frac{1}{L^2z}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 16.

Fix \(L\ge2\), so \(L>0\).

  1. Write \[Q_L(z)=1+\sum_{j=1}^L q_{L,j}z^j, \qquad q_{L,j}=[z^j]Q_L .\] The shifted Chebyshev expansion used for \(Q_L(z)=T_L(1-2z)\) gives \[\|Q_L\|_1 =1+\sum_{j=1}^L |q_{L,j}| =T_L(3).\] The standard growth bound for Chebyshev polynomials at \(3\) gives \(T_L(3)\le 6^L\), and hence \[\|Q_L\|_1\le 6^L\le 7^L .\] For the quotient polynomial, Lemma 15 gives \[\|G_L\|_1\le 6^L .\] Since \(L\ge2\), \[2\|G_L\|_1 \le 2\cdot 6^L \le 2^L7^L =14^L .\] This proves the two coefficient bounds.

  2. Fix \(a\in\{0,1\}\), \(t>0\), \(B>0\), and \(s_0,s_1\ge0\), and assume \(L\le tB\). Put \[\mathscr D=tB,\qquad p=s_0+s_1,\qquad \eta=1+\frac pB .\] Then \(\mathscr D>0\), \(\mathscr D(s_b/B)=ts_b\) for \(b=0,1\), and \(\eta\ge1\). Let \[\mathscr I_L=\{j\in\mathbb N:[z^j]G_L\ne0\}.\] To identify this index set, write \[Q_L(z)=1+q_{L,1}z+\sum_{r=2}^L q_{L,r}z^r, \qquad q_{L,1}=-2L^2.\] The definitions of \(H_L\) and \(E_L\) give the scaled identity \[zE_L(z)=\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}.\] Using the displayed value of \(q_{L,1}\), this identity gives \[E_L(z)=1-\sum_{r=2}^L\frac{q_{L,r}}{2L^2}z^{r-1},\] and the quotient definition of \(G_L\) gives \[G_L(z)=\frac{1-E_L(z)}z =\sum_{r=2}^L\frac{q_{L,r}}{2L^2}z^{r-2}.\] Thus \[\mathscr I_L\subseteq\{0,\ldots,L-2\},\] with the assertion empty if the displayed sum has no nonzero terms, and every \(j\in\mathscr I_L\) satisfies \(j+1\le L\). For \(K=(K_0,K_1)\), with \(K_a=K_1\) when \(a=1\) and \(K_a=K_0\) when \(a=0\), define \[X_j(K) = \frac{(K_a)_{j+1}}{\mathscr D^{j+1}} + \frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}, \qquad j\in\mathscr I_L .\] If \(\mathscr I_L\) is empty, the following sum is zero. With \(K_b\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_b)\) independently, the identities \(\mathscr D(s_b/B)=ts_b\) give the factorial lift as \[\widehat W_{a,L} = \sum_{j\in\mathscr I_L} [z^j]G_L\,X_j(K).\]

    For the moment bound, first use the one-dimensional Poisson falling-factorial estimate: if \(\mathfrak N_z\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\mathscr D z)\), \(z\ge0\), and \(r,v\le L\), then \[\mathbb E\frac{(\mathfrak N_z)_r(\mathfrak N_z)_v}{\mathscr D^{r+v}} = \sum_{h=0}^{\min(r,v)} \binom rh\binom vh h!\, \frac{(\mathscr D z)^{r+v-h}}{\mathscr D^{r+v}}.\] For \(0\le h\le\min(r,v)\), the corresponding summand satisfies \[\binom rh\binom vh h!\, \frac{(\mathscr D z)^{r+v-h}}{\mathscr D^{r+v}} \le \frac{(rv)^h}{h!\mathscr D^h}(1+z)^{r+v} \le \frac{L^h}{h!}(1+z)^{r+v},\] because \(rv/\mathscr D\le L\), which follows from \(r,v\le L\) and \(L\le\mathscr D\), and because \(z^{r+v-h}\le(1+z)^{r+v}\). Therefore \[\mathbb E\frac{(\mathfrak N_z)_r(\mathfrak N_z)_v}{\mathscr D^{r+v}} \le \sum_{h=0}^{L}\frac{L^h}{h!}(1+z)^{r+v} \le 3^L(1+z)^{r+v},\] where the last inequality is the elementary finite-series bound \(\sum_{h=0}^L L^h/h!\le3^L\).

    This estimate gives the same-arm square bound. For every \(0\le r\le L\), integrating out the unused arm in the product law and applying the preceding estimate with \(z=s_a/B\) and orders \((r,r)\) gives \[\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_r}{\mathscr D^r}\right)^2 \le 3^L\left(1+\frac{s_a}{B}\right)^{2r}.\] Since \(1+s_a/B\le\eta\), \(\eta\ge1\), and \(2r\le2L\), \[\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_r}{\mathscr D^r}\right)^2 \le 3^L\eta^{2L}.\] In particular, for \(j\in\mathscr I_L\), using \(j+1\le L\), \[\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_{j+1}}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2 \le 3^L\eta^{2L}.\]

    The cross-arm square bound follows by the product structure. For \(j\in\mathscr I_L\), independence and \((K_{1-a})_1=K_{1-a}\) give \[\mathbb E\left(\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2 = \mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^j}\right)^2 \mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_{1-a})_1}{\mathscr D}\right)^2.\] Applying the one-dimensional estimate to arm \(a\) with orders \((j,j)\) and to arm \(1-a\) with orders \((1,1)\) yields \[\mathbb E\left(\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2 \le 3^L\left(1+\frac{s_a}{B}\right)^{2j} \cdot 3^L\left(1+\frac{s_{1-a}}{B}\right)^2.\] Now \(1+s_a/B\le\eta\), \(1+s_{1-a}/B\le\eta\), \(\eta\ge1\), and \(2j+2\le2L\). Hence \[\left(1+\frac{s_a}{B}\right)^{2j} \left(1+\frac{s_{1-a}}{B}\right)^2 \le \eta^{2j+2} \le \eta^{2L},\] and therefore \[\mathbb E\left(\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2 \le 9^L\eta^{2L}.\] Since \((\xi+\upsilon)^2\le2\xi^2+2\upsilon^2\), \[\mathbb E X_j(K)^2 \le 2\cdot 3^L\eta^{2L}+2\cdot 9^L\eta^{2L} \le 36^L\eta^{2L} = \bigl(6^L\eta^L\bigr)^2 .\] The finite-sum \(L^2\) inequality \[\mathbb E\left(\sum_{j\in\mathscr I_L} c_jX_j\right)^2 \le \left(\sum_{j\in\mathscr I_L}|c_j|\right)^2 \max_{j\in\mathscr I_L}\mathbb E X_j^2\] therefore yields \[\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L}^2 \le \|G_L\|_1^2\bigl(6^L\eta^L\bigr)^2 \le (6^L)^2\bigl(6^L\eta^L\bigr)^2 = 1296^L\eta^{2L}.\] Because \(1296\le7056\), \[\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L}^2 \le 7056^L\left(1+\frac pB\right)^{2L}.\] If \(p\le B\), then \(\eta\le2\), and hence \[\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L}^2 \le 1296^L2^{2L} = 5184^L \le 7056^L .\] This proves both second-moment assertions.

  3. Let \(z\in[0,1]\). The scaled identity for the continuation is \[zE_L(z)=(2L^2)^{-1}\{1-Q_L(z)\}= \frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}.\] At \(z=0\), the derivative identity \(T_L'(1)=L^2\) gives \(E_L(0)=1\). For \(z>0\), put \[\theta=\arccos(1-2z).\] Then \(Q_L(z)=\cos(L\theta)\), so \(|Q_L(z)|\le1\). Hence \(1-Q_L(z)\ge0\), and the scaled identity gives \(E_L(z)\ge0\). Also \[1-Q_L(z) = 1-\cos(L\theta) \le L^2(1-\cos\theta) = 2L^2z,\] so \(zE_L(z)\le z\), whence \(E_L(z)\le1\). Finally, \(|Q_L(z)|\le1\) gives \(1-Q_L(z)\le2\), and the normalization by \(2L^2\) in the scaled identity gives \[zE_L(z) = \frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2} \le \frac1{L^2},\] and division by \(z>0\) gives \[E_L(z)\le\frac1{L^2z}.\] The quotient definition gives the polynomial identity \[zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1\] for every real \(z\), and therefore on \([0,1]\).

∎

Lemma 16 supplies the numerical coefficient bounds used to instantiate Lemma 5 in the calibrated estimator. In particular, the constant \(7056\) gives the explicit factorial-lift second-moment scale that appears in the finite calibration of Algorithm 1.

Common-marginal lower bounds

The converse in Theorem 3 rests on priors whose treatment–covariate marginal is shared across branches while their average treatment effect centers are separated. This appendix collects the finite approximation and transfer statements that make that construction compatible with the annotation experiment in Definition 9. The first object is a two-cell calculation that isolates the statistical issue in its smallest form.

Definition 27 [def:finite-small-instance] (Finite small instance \(\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}\)).

Let \[\epsilon=\frac14,\qquad d=2,\qquad p_1=p_2=\frac12,\qquad e_1=e_2=\frac12,\qquad \mu_{01}=\mu_{02}=0.\] For \(n=m=1\), independence of the samples gives \[\mathbb E\!\left[ 2\mathbf 1\{\mathcal L_1=((1,1,1))\} \left(\mathbf 1\{\mathcal U_1=((1,0))\}+\mathbf 1\{\mathcal U_1=((1,1))\}\right) \right] =2q_{11}p_1,\] where \(q_{11}\) is the outcome-marked mass for treatment-covariate cell \((1,1)\). At \(\mu_{11}=1\), this equals \(1/4\), while the treated-cell target is \[p_1\mu_{11}=\frac12.\] Define \(\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}\) to put unit mass on the law with \[\mu_{11}=\mu_{12}=0,\] and define \(\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}\) to put unit mass on the law with \[\mu_{11}=1,\qquad \mu_{12}=0.\] Under both priors, \[P_{XA}(x,a)=\frac14 \quad\text{for all }(x,a),\] whereas \[\tau(P)=0\] under \(\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}\), and \[\tau(P)=\frac12\] under \(\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}\). Consequently, the auxiliary laws agree for every \(m\), and the total variation distance between the two labeled laws is \[1-\left(\frac34\right)^n.\] These identities constitute \(\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}\).

⊢ Lean

The instance in Definition 27 makes concrete the role of outcome labels. The two priors \(\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}\) and \(\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}\) share the same treatment–covariate marginal \(P_{XA}\), so auxiliary observations identify the common table while the target separation remains attached to the outcome-marked mass \(q_{ax}\). The calculation \(\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}\) is finite, but it has the same common-marginal structure used in the high-dimensional lower bound.

The next statement supplies the approximation-duality ingredient. It constructs a signed finite measure on a short interval whose first \(L\) moments vanish while the inverse-weight functional retains a fixed gap. This is the same polynomial approximation mechanism that appears in nonsmooth functional lower bounds for discrete distributions (Jiao et al., 2015; Wu et al., 2016; Valiant et al., 2017; Orlitsky et al., 2016).

Lemma 17 [lem:finite-alternation-duality] (Finite alternation duality).

Fix an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), and set \[\kappa=\frac{1-2\epsilon}{\epsilon}.\] There exist constants \(\gamma_\epsilon>0\) and \(c_\epsilon>0\) such that, for every integer \(L\ge 2\) and every \(B>0\), with \[a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},\] one has \(a/\kappa\le B\), and there are distinct points \(p_0,\ldots,p_{L+1}\in [a/\kappa,B]\) and real weights \(w_0,\ldots,w_{L+1}\) satisfying \[\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|w_i|=1,\qquad \sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i p_i^j=0\quad\text{for every }0\le j\le L,\] and \[c_\epsilon\le \sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i\,\frac{p_i}{p_i+a}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 17.

  1. Set \[D_\epsilon=\frac{\kappa}{(1+\kappa)(2+\kappa)},\qquad \gamma_\epsilon=\min\left\{\frac{\kappa}{4},\frac{D_\epsilon\kappa}{32}\right\},\qquad c_\epsilon=\frac{D_\epsilon}{8}.\] Since \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), we have \(\kappa>0\). Hence \(D_\epsilon>0\), \(\gamma_\epsilon>0\), and \(c_\epsilon>0\). Also \(D_\epsilon<1\), because \[\kappa<(1+\kappa)(2+\kappa)\] for \(\kappa>0\).

  2. Fix \(L\ge2\) and \(B>0\), and put \[a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2}.\] Then \(a>0\). Since \(\gamma_\epsilon\le \kappa/4\) and \(L^2\ge4\), \[\frac{a}{\kappa} =\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{\kappa L^2} \le B.\] Moreover, \[\frac{a}{\kappa}\le \frac{B}{16},\] because \(\gamma_\epsilon\le \kappa/4\) and \(L^2\ge4\). In particular \(a/\kappa<B\).

  3. Let \[I=\left[\frac{a}{\kappa},B\right], \qquad f(p)=\frac{p}{p+a}.\] The pole \(-a\) lies to the left of \(I\), since \(a>0\) and \(a/\kappa>0\). Thus \(f\) is continuous on \(I\). Let \(E\) be the best uniform error for approximating \(f\) on \(I\) by polynomials of degree at most \(L\). The finite alternation theorem supplies an alternation dual certificate: distinct nodes in \(I\), normalized weights annihilating all monomials of degree at most \(L\), and an associated degree-\(L\) best approximating polynomial.

  4. We claim \[c_\epsilon\le E.\] Suppose instead that \(E<c_\epsilon\). Define \[p_-=\frac{a}{\kappa},\qquad p_+=\frac{2a}{\kappa}.\] Then \(p_-<p_+\), and both points belong to \(I\). Indeed \(p_-=a/\kappa\), while \[p_+=\frac{2\gamma_\epsilon B}{\kappa L^2}\le B\] because \(\gamma_\epsilon\le \kappa\) and \(L^2\ge4\). At these two points, \[f(p_-)=\frac{1}{1+\kappa},\qquad f(p_+)=\frac{2}{2+\kappa},\] so \[f(p_+)-f(p_-) =\frac{\kappa}{(1+\kappa)(2+\kappa)} =D_\epsilon.\] Let \(Q\) be the best approximating polynomial from the alternation certificate. For every \(p\in I\), \[|f(p)-Q(p)|\le E.\] Consequently, \[D_\epsilon-2E \le Q(p_+)-Q(p_-).\] By the mean-value theorem there is a point \(\xi\in[p_-,p_+]\subset I\) such that \[Q'(\xi)=\frac{Q(p_+)-Q(p_-)}{p_+-p_-},\] and therefore \[\frac{D_\epsilon-2E}{p_+-p_-}\le |Q'(\xi)|.\] Since \(E<c_\epsilon=D_\epsilon/8\), the numerator is positive.

  5. On \(I\), the target satisfies \(0\le f(p)\le1\). Combining this with \(|f(p)-Q(p)|\le E\) gives \[|Q(p)|\le 1+E\qquad (p\in I),\] or equivalently \[\|Q\|_{\infty,I}\le 1+E.\] Markov’s inequality on the interval \(I=[a/\kappa,B]\), applied to the degree-\(L\) polynomial \(Q\), gives \[\|Q'\|_{\infty,I} \le \frac{2L^2}{B-a/\kappa}\,\|Q\|_{\infty,I} \le \frac{2L^2}{B-a/\kappa}(1+E).\] Because \(a/\kappa\le B/16\), we have \(B/2\le B-a/\kappa\). Also \(E<c_\epsilon<1\). Hence \[|Q'(\xi)| \le \frac{2L^2}{B-a/\kappa}(1+E) \le \frac{2L^2}{B/2}\cdot2 = \frac{8L^2}{B}.\] Combining this with the previous lower bound and using \[p_+-p_-=\frac{a}{\kappa} =\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{\kappa L^2}\] yields \[\frac{(D_\epsilon-2E)\kappa L^2}{\gamma_\epsilon B} \le \frac{8L^2}{B}.\] After cancellation, \[(D_\epsilon-2E)\kappa\le 8\gamma_\epsilon.\] But \(E<D_\epsilon/8\), so \(D_\epsilon-2E>3D_\epsilon/4\), while \[8\gamma_\epsilon\le \frac{D_\epsilon\kappa}{4}.\] This is impossible. Therefore \(c_\epsilon\le E\).

  6. Let the alternation certificate have nodes \(p_0,\ldots,p_{L+1}\in I\) and weights \(\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_{L+1}\). They satisfy \[\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|\alpha_i|=1, \qquad \sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i p_i^j=0\quad(0\le j\le L),\] and \[\left|\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i f(p_i)\right|=E.\] Choose \[s= \begin{cases} 1,&\text{if }\sum_i\alpha_i f(p_i)\ge0,\\ -1,&\text{if }\sum_i\alpha_i f(p_i)<0, \end{cases} \qquad w_i=s\alpha_i .\] Then the nodes remain distinct and lie in \(I\), and \[\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|w_i| = \sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|\alpha_i| =1.\] For every \(0\le j\le L\), \[\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i p_i^j = s\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i p_i^j =0.\] Finally, \[\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i\frac{p_i}{p_i+a} = s\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i f(p_i) = \left|\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i f(p_i)\right| = E \ge c_\epsilon.\] These nodes and weights give the required finite moment dual certificate on \([a/\kappa,B]\).

∎

Lemma 17 turns polynomial indistinguishability into target separation. The moment cancellations control likelihood differences after Poissonization, while the final display gives a fixed gap for the inverse-weight expression that enters the treatment-effect contrast.

The rare-cell prior packages this dual certificate into a common-marginal experiment. For the transfer arguments, it is useful to state the construction as a recipe separate from any particular choice of sample intensities. The next definition records the raw rare-cell masses, the two branch laws, and the raw target centers that the lower-bound reductions manipulate; the later uniform-intensity lemma supplies the exact Poisson witness actually used by the converse proof.

Definition 28 [synth_20] (Common-marginal rare-cell recipe).

A common-marginal rare-cell recipe with \(k\) rare cells at scale \((a,B,L)\) is a finite random construction \(\mathcal R\) consisting of a generator law \(\operatorname{generator}_{\mathcal R}\) for a latent draw \(w\), together with two branch laws indexed by \(b\in\{0,1\}\). For each generated \(w\), the recipe specifies rare-cell raw masses \(p_i(w)\in[0,B]\), \(i=1,\ldots,k\), branch-specific outcome regressions, and any reservoir mass needed to normalize the construction. The two branches have the same treatment–covariate raw masses and treatment propensities, so after normalization they induce the same \(P_{XA}\) table, while their outcome regressions may differ.

For each branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), let \(P_b(w)\) be the normalized law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) induced by the recipe, and let \[\operatorname{rawTarget}_{\mathcal R}(b,w)\] be the unnormalized target contribution assigned by the rare cells in branch \(b\). The branch center is \[\theta_b=\int \operatorname{rawTarget}_{\mathcal R}(b,w)\, d\operatorname{generator}_{\mathcal R}(w),\] and the raw-center separation is \(\Delta=|\theta_1-\theta_0|\). The recipe induces, for each branch \(b\), the mixture experiment obtained by first drawing \(w\sim\operatorname{generator}_{\mathcal R}\), then sampling from \(P_b(w)\) for labeled observations and from the common marginal \(P_{b,XA}(w)\) for auxiliary observations. Its raw Poisson version uses labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\) with the same generated law \(P_b(w)\), so the treatment–covariate component is common across the two branches and the likelihood discrepancy is carried by the labeled outcome-bearing component.

Definition 28 fixes the bookkeeping used by the lower-bound priors. The random raw masses determine a common treatment–covariate table, and the branch-specific outcome regressions create a raw-center separation before normalization. This separation is the quantity later converted into minimax risk.

The raw Poisson comparison enters several concentration and transfer statements, so the notation for the branch predictive laws is named explicitly before those estimates.

Lemma 18 [lem:common-marginal-recipe-target-concentration] (Prior target concentration).

Fix \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), a common-marginal calibration at overlap level \(\epsilon\) with dual-gap constant \(c_\epsilon\), and parameters \(n,m,d,L,k\in\mathbb N\) and \(B,a,u,v,C,\rho\in\mathbb R\). Let \(\mathcal R\) be a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with atom-generator measure \(\Gamma\), branch laws \(P_b(w)\), raw targets \(r_b(w)\), raw total mass \(S(w)\), and branch priors \[\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}, \qquad \theta_b=\int r_b(w)\,d\Gamma(w), \qquad b\in\{0,1\}.\] Assume the recipe bounds hold at intensities \((u,v)\) with constants \((C,\rho)\) and dual gap \(c_\epsilon\):

  • (Finite-atom construction.) The recipe uses the finite-atom construction.

  • (Model support.) Each prior is supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) of Definition 6.

  • (Common table.) The two priors induce the same law of the random treatment-covariate table.

  • (Separation.) The raw centers satisfy \[c_\epsilon k a\le |\theta_1-\theta_0|.\]

  • (Mass and variance bounds.) Under the usual measurability and integrability conditions, \[\int S(w)\,d\Gamma(w)=1,\qquad \operatorname{Var}_\Gamma(S)\le CkBa,\qquad \max_{b\in\{0,1\}}\operatorname{Var}_\Gamma(r_b)\le CkBa.\]

  • (Mixture proximity.) The two raw mixture laws at labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\) have total variation distance at most \[Cuka\rho^L.\]

Then for every branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and every \(\Delta\) with \(0<\Delta\le2\), the branch prior concentrates its average treatment effect \(\tau(P)\), defined in Definition 8, around its raw center: \[\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\} \le \frac{512\,CkBa}{\Delta^2}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 18.

Fix \(b\in\{0,1\}\). Define the two exceptional events in the atom space by \[\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}} := \left\{w:\frac{\Delta}{16}<|r_b(w)-\theta_b|\right\}, \qquad \mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}} := \left\{w:\frac{\Delta}{16}<|S(w)-1|\right\}.\] We first record the normalization implication used below. Outside a \(\Gamma\)-null set the recipe gives \[r_b(w)=S(w)\tau(P_b(w)), \qquad S(w)>0.\] Suppose \[\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|, \qquad w\notin \mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}.\] The second condition means \[|r_b(w)-\theta_b|\le \frac{\Delta}{16}, \qquad |S(w)-1|\le \frac{\Delta}{16}.\] The raw-center bound supplied by the recipe normalization is \[|\theta_b|\le 1.\] Hence \[|S(w)\theta_b-\theta_b| = |(S(w)-1)\theta_b| \le \frac{\Delta}{16}.\] Using \(r_b(w)=S(w)\tau(P_b(w))\), the triangle inequality gives \[S(w)|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b| = |S(w)\tau(P_b(w))-S(w)\theta_b| \le \frac{\Delta}{8}.\] Also \(|S(w)-1|\le\Delta/16\) and \(\Delta\le2\) imply \[S(w)\ge 1-\frac{\Delta}{16}\ge \frac78 .\] Therefore \[\frac78\,|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b| \le S(w)|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b| \le \frac{\Delta}{8},\] which contradicts \(\Delta/4<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|\). Thus, for \(\Gamma\)-almost every \(w\), \[\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b| \quad\Longrightarrow\quad w\in\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}} .\]

By the definition of the branch prior as the pushforward of \(\Gamma\) under \(P_b\), with the two Boolean branches handled separately, \[\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\} = \Gamma\!\left\{w:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|\right\}.\] The implication above yields \[\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\} \le \Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}).\]

We next bound the two exceptional probabilities. Chebyshev’s inequality, applied under \(\Gamma\) with mean \[\theta_b=\int r_b(w)\,d\Gamma(w),\] and the assumed variance bound \[\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(r_b)\le CkBa,\] gives \[\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}) \le \frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2}.\] Similarly, since \[\int S(w)\,d\Gamma(w)=1, \qquad \operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)\le CkBa,\] Chebyshev’s inequality gives \[\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}) \le \frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2}.\]

Subadditivity of measure gives \[\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}) \le \Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}})+\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}).\] Combining the preceding displays, \[\begin{aligned} \Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\} &\le \frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2} + \frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2} \\ &= \frac{512\,CkBa}{\Delta^2}, \end{aligned}\] where the final equality uses \(\Delta>0\). This is the desired bound for the fixed branch \(b\), and hence for both branches.

∎

Definition 29 separates the prior randomness in the raw masses from the Poisson sampling randomness. With this notation, total variation between the two branch mixtures measures the experiment-level testing difficulty after the common treatment–covariate table has been integrated over.

The next two lemmas isolate the normalization estimates used by the transfer. The first converts raw target control into concentration of the average treatment effect, and the second records the corresponding lower-tail control for the raw scale.

Definition 29 [synth_21] (Raw Poisson prior-predictive mixture laws).

Fix a common-marginal rare-cell recipe \(\mathcal R\) with atom generator \(\Gamma\), branch laws \(P_b(w)\) for \(b\in\{0,1\}\), and branch priors \(\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}\). At labeled and auxiliary Poisson intensities \(u\) and \(v\), define \(\mathsf Q_b\) to be the branch-\(b\) raw prior-predictive law obtained by first drawing \(w\sim\Gamma\), forming the raw branch law \(P_b(w)\), and then sampling the independent raw Poisson experiment with labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\) from that raw law and its treatment–covariate marginal. Equivalently, \[\mathsf Q_b = \int \Bigl\{ \operatorname{PoiExp}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{raw}}\bigl(P_b(w)\bigr) \Bigr\}\,d\Gamma(w) = \int \operatorname{PoiExp}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{raw}}(P)\,d\Pi_b(P), \qquad b\in\{0,1\},\] where \(\operatorname{PoiExp}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{raw}}(P)\) denotes the product Poisson law for the outcome-labeled raw cell counts at intensity \(u\) and the auxiliary treatment–covariate raw cell counts at intensity \(v\).

Lemma 19 [lem:common-marginal-recipe-scale-tail] (Small-scale prior tail).

Fix \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), a common-marginal calibration with positive constants including dual-gap constant \(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\), integers \(n,m,d,L,k\ge0\), and reals \(B,a,u,v,C,\rho\). Let \(\mathcal R\) be a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with atom-generator \(\Gamma\), branch laws \(P_b(w)\), branch priors \(\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}\), raw total mass \(S(w)\), raw targets \(r_b(w)\), and measurable raw scale \(s_{\mathcal R}\) satisfying \(s_{\mathcal R}(P_b(w))=S(w)\) for \(\Gamma\)-almost every \(w\).

Assume that the recipe bounds hold for \(\mathcal R\) at \((u,v,c_\epsilon,C,\rho)\):

  • (Finite-atom construction.) The recipe uses the finite-atom construction.

  • (Model support and common table.) The two priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) from Definition 6, and they induce the same distribution of the random treatment-covariate table \(P_{XA}\).

  • (Center separation.) If \(\theta_b\) denotes the raw prior center for branch \(b\), then \[c_\epsilon k a \le |\theta_1-\theta_0|.\]

  • (Mass and variance bounds.) Under the usual measurability and integrability conditions, \[\int S\,d\Gamma=1,\qquad \operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)\le CkBa,\qquad \max\{\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(r_0),\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(r_1)\}\le CkBa.\]

  • (Mixture distance.) The two raw Poisson mixture laws generated by the recipe satisfy \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)\le Cuka\rho^L.\]

Then, for each branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), \[\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}<\frac12\right\} \le 4CkBa .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 19.

  1. Define the mass-tail event on the atom space by \[\mathsf{MassTail} := \Bigl\{w:\frac12<|S(w)-1|\Bigr\}.\] The mass assumptions give \[\int S\,d\Gamma=1, \qquad \operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)\le CkBa.\] Since \(\Gamma\) is a probability law, Chebyshev’s inequality at radius \(1/2\) yields \[\Gamma(\mathsf{MassTail}) = \Gamma\Bigl\{w:\frac12<|S(w)-1|\Bigr\} \le \frac{\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)}{(1/2)^2} \le 4CkBa .\]

  2. For a fixed branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), set \[\mathsf{SmallScale}_b := \Bigl\{P:\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}<\frac12\Bigr\}.\] Because \(\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}\), \[\Pi_b(\mathsf{SmallScale}_b) = \Gamma\{w:P_b(w)\in\mathsf{SmallScale}_b\}.\] For \(\Gamma\)-almost every \(w\), the recipe supplies both identities \[s_{\mathcal R}(P_b(w))=S(w) \qquad\text{and}\qquad 0<S(w).\] On this full-measure set, \(P_b(w)\in\mathsf{SmallScale}_b\) implies \[S(w)=\max\{S(w),0\}<\frac12.\] Hence \(S(w)-1<-1/2\), and therefore \[|S(w)-1|=1-S(w)>\frac12.\] Thus \[\{w:P_b(w)\in\mathsf{SmallScale}_b\} \subseteq \mathsf{MassTail} \quad\text{up to a \(\Gamma\)-null set,}\] and consequently \[\Pi_b(\mathsf{SmallScale}_b)\le \Gamma(\mathsf{MassTail}).\]

  3. The preceding containment is applied to the two branch cases separately: for \(b=0\) it uses \[\Pi_0=\Gamma\circ P_0^{-1},\] and for \(b=1\) it uses \[\Pi_1=\Gamma\circ P_1^{-1}.\] Combining the appropriate branch identity with the bound from the first step gives, for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\), \[\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}<\frac12\right\} \le \Gamma(\mathsf{MassTail}) \le 4CkBa .\]

∎

Lemmas 18 and 19 make the raw construction usable after normalization. The target concentration bound relates the branch centers \(\theta_b\) to the actual functional values under the induced priors, while the scale-tail bound keeps the normalizing mass away from the region where raw-to-normalized conversion would amplify the rare-cell perturbation.

The next transfer lemma applies the recipe at fixed Poisson intensities large enough to dominate the fixed sample sizes. Its assumptions combine a total-variation bound with a scale condition that controls the random normalization.

Lemma 20 [lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer] (Recipe transfer bound).

Let \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), where \(\epsilon\) is the overlap level. Fix a common-marginal calibration at level \(\epsilon\), consisting of positive constants including \(\gamma_\epsilon\) and dual gap \(\delta_\epsilon\le 1/4\), a constant \(C_\epsilon>0\), and a real number \(\rho_\epsilon\). Let \(n,m,d,L,k\) be nonnegative integers, let \(B,a\in\mathbb R\), and let \(\mathcal R\) be a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with \(k\) rare cells at scale \((a,B,L)\). For each branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), define the raw prior center \[\theta_b=\int \operatorname{rawTarget}_{\mathcal R}(b,w)\,dG_{\mathcal R}(w),\] where \(G_{\mathcal R}\) is the atom-generator measure of the recipe, and put \[\Delta=|\theta_1-\theta_0|.\] Assume:

  • (Sample and dimension.) \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 2\).

  • (Poisson-prior data.) With \[u=65536\,n \qquad\text{and}\qquad v=65536\,(n+m),\] the recipe has bandwidth \(B>0\), shift \[a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},\] rare-shift size \[ka\le \delta_\epsilon,\] and the recipe intensities agree with \(u\) and \(v\).

  • (Bandwidth and likelihood bounds.) The realized scale satisfies \[(u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L,\] where \(b_\epsilon\) is the calibration’s uniform-bandwidth constant, and the recipe satisfies the common-marginal Poisson-prior bounds at gap \(\delta_\epsilon\), intensity pair \((u,v)\), variance constant \(C_\epsilon\), and decay constant \(\rho_\epsilon\).

  • (Mixture affinity.) The two prior-predictive raw Poisson-count laws generated by the two recipe priors and the recipe’s experiment kernel have total variation distance at most \[\frac18 .\]

  • (Variance scale.) The recipe scale obeys \[kBa\le \frac{\Delta^2}{65536\,C_\epsilon}.\]

Then the minimax risk of Definition 11 satisfies \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 20.

Let \[\Pi_b:=\text{the branch-\(b\) prior induced by \(\mathcal R\)},\qquad \theta_b:=\int r_b(w)\,d\Gamma(w),\qquad b\in\{0,1\},\] where \(\Gamma\) is the atom-generator measure and \(r_b\) is the raw target of the recipe. Thus \[\Delta=|\theta_1-\theta_0|.\] Also put \[u:=65536\,n,\qquad v:=65536\,(n+m),\] and, for every law \(P\), define the totalized nonnegative recipe scale \[s_+(P):=\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}.\]

  1. The witness bounds imply \[|\theta_0|\le 1,\qquad |\theta_1|\le 1.\] Indeed, each \(\theta_b\) is the \(\Gamma\)-mean of the raw target, the raw target is \(S(w)\tau(P_b(w))\), \(|\tau(P_b(w))|\le1\) on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), and the recipe bounds give \(\int S\,d\Gamma=1\). Hence \[0\le\Delta\le 2.\] The intensities satisfy \(u\ge0\) and \(v\ge0\). The branch priors are probability measures, and the support part of the witness gives the required nonempty model class.

  2. Let \(\mathscr K_{\rm raw}\) be the ordered raw Poisson-count experiment which, at law \(P\), draws independent ordered Poisson samples from the labeled mark law and the auxiliary mark law with intensities \[u\,s_+(P) \qquad\text{and}\qquad v\,s_+(P).\] Let \(\mathsf Q_b\) be the prior-predictive law obtained from \(\Pi_b\) through \(\mathscr K_{\rm raw}\). We now identify this law as a Markov image of the raw count law in the statement. Let \(\mathscr K_{\rm cnt}\) denote the recipe’s raw Poisson-count kernel before ordering. Let \(\operatorname{reindex}\) be the deterministic map that rewrites a raw count table as labeled-mark and auxiliary-mark count functions, and let \(\operatorname{order}\) be the paired-histogram kernel that, conditional on those two count functions, orders the corresponding multiset of labeled marks and the corresponding multiset of auxiliary marks uniformly and independently. Put \[\operatorname{Rec}:=\operatorname{order}\circ\operatorname{reindex} .\] For every law \(P\), the raw count kernel factors through this reconstruction as \[\operatorname{Rec}\circ \mathscr K_{\rm cnt}(P) = \mathscr K_{\rm raw}(P).\] Indeed, after the deterministic reindexing, the labeled and auxiliary count functions have independent Poisson coordinates with means \(u\,s_+(P)\) times the labeled mark law and \(v\,s_+(P)\) times the auxiliary mark law; ordering each histogram gives exactly the ordered independent Poisson samples with those two intensities. Therefore, for each branch, \[\mathcal R_{\rm raw,b} :=\int \mathscr K_{\rm cnt}(P)\,d\Pi_b(P) \qquad\text{satisfies}\qquad \mathsf Q_b =\int \mathscr K_{\rm raw}(P)\,d\Pi_b(P) =(\operatorname{Rec})_{\#}\mathcal R_{\rm raw,b}.\] Total variation contracts under a Markov kernel, and the assumed raw mixture affinity gives \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1) \le \operatorname{TV}(\mathcal R_{\rm raw,0},\mathcal R_{\rm raw,1}) \le \frac18 .\]

  3. Set \[\alpha_{\rm floor}:=\frac1{100}.\] The fixed experiment has the parametric two-point floor \[\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] To see this, take \[\delta_n:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n}.\] Choose a cell \(x_\star\in[d]\). Under both laws below, let \(X=x_\star\) almost surely and let the treatment propensity be \(1/2\). Let the control outcome mean be \(1/2\) under both laws; let the treated outcome mean be \(1/2\) for \(P_{\rm par,0}\) and \(1/2+\delta_n\) for \(P_{\rm par,1}\). Since \(n\ge1\), \[0\le\delta_n\le\frac25,\] so these are valid binary outcome laws, and since \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), both belong to \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). Their treatment-covariate marginals agree, their average treatment effects are \[\tau(P_{\rm par,0})=0, \qquad \tau(P_{\rm par,1})=\delta_n,\] and hence the auxiliary samples have identical laws. For the labeled samples, the standard Bernoulli product testing bound applies because \[n\cdot \frac{(1/2)\delta_n^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)} = \frac{8}{25} \le \log 2,\] and therefore \[\operatorname{TV}\!\bigl(P_{\rm par,0}^{\otimes n},P_{\rm par,1}^{\otimes n}\bigr) \le\frac12.\] Le Cam’s two-point bound with separation \(\delta_n\) yields \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \ge \frac{(\delta_n/2)^2}{4} = \frac1{100\,n}.\] For the fixed-sample decision problem with the class-gated clipped squared loss, let \[\mathfrak R_{\rm dec} := \inf_{\psi} \sup_P \int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi)\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P),\] where the infimum is over measurable fixed-sample rules, \(\mathscr K_{\rm fix}\) is the fixed labeled-and-auxiliary experiment, and \[\ell_\epsilon(P,z) := \begin{cases} \bigl(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(z)-\tau(P)\bigr)^2,& P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon},\\ 0,& P\notin\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}. \end{cases}\] We use the following elementary comparison between Definition 11 and the class-gated decision problem: \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \le \mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.\] Indeed, every measurable fixed-sample rule \(\psi\) induces the measurable estimator \[\widetilde T_\psi:=\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}\circ\psi .\] For every \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), the class-gated loss evaluates the same clipped rule, so \[\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y) = E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[\bigl(\widetilde T_\psi-\tau(P)\bigr)^2\right].\] Thus the worst-case on-class decision risk of \(\psi\) is exactly the worst-case mean-squared risk of \(\widetilde T_\psi\). Since \(\widetilde T_\psi\) is one admissible estimator in \(\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\), Definition 11 is no larger than that worst-case risk; taking the infimum over \(\psi\) gives the displayed comparison. Combining it with the parametric floor gives \[\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n} \le \mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.\]

  4. If \(\Delta=0\), then the desired lower bound is \(0\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\), which follows from the preceding floor since \(n\ge1\). Hence assume for the rest of the proof that \[0<\Delta.\] The variance-scale hypothesis and \(C_\epsilon>0\) give \[C_\epsilon kBa\le \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.\] By Lemma 18, for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\), \[\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\} \le \frac{512\,C_\epsilon kBa}{\Delta^2} \le \frac1{128} \le \frac1{16}.\] The assumed common-marginal Poisson-prior bounds also include the raw-center separation \[\delta_\epsilon k a\le |\theta_1-\theta_0|=\Delta,\] which is the center-separation hypothesis required below. By Lemma 19, again for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\), \[\Pi_b\{P:s_+(P)<1/2\} \le 4C_\epsilon kBa \le \frac{\Delta^2}{16384}.\]

  5. The two fuzzy hypotheses with centers \(\theta_0,\theta_1\), radius \(\Delta/4\), prior tail bounds \(1/16,1/16\), and predictive total variation bound \(1/8\) give the raw lower bound as follows. For a measurable raw rule \(\varphi\), define \[\mathcal B_b(\varphi) := \int^{+} \int^{+} \ell_\epsilon(P,\varphi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm raw}(P)(y)\,d\Pi_b(P) \in\overline{\mathbb R}_+, \qquad b\in\{0,1\}.\] These are the branch Bayes decision risks for the class-gated clipped loss. Since the two branch priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), the integrand on the support is \[\ell_\epsilon(P,\varphi(y)) = \bigl(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(\varphi(y))-\tau(P)\bigr)^2.\] Let \(\mathsf Q_b\) be the branch predictive laws from step 2, and first suppose \(\theta_0\le\theta_1\). Put \[\theta_{\rm mid}:=\frac{\theta_0+\theta_1}{2}, \qquad \mathsf A_\varphi := \bigl\{y:\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(\varphi(y))\ge\theta_{\rm mid}\bigr\},\] and define the good prior events \[\mathsf G_0:=\bigl\{P:|\tau(P)-\theta_0|\le\Delta/4\bigr\}, \qquad \mathsf G_1:=\bigl\{P:|\tau(P)-\theta_1|\le\Delta/4\bigr\}.\] Because \(\theta_1-\theta_0=|\theta_1-\theta_0|\ge\Delta\), on \(\mathsf G_0\cap\mathsf A_\varphi\) the clipped decision is at least \(\Delta/4\) above \(\tau(P)\), while on \(\mathsf G_1\cap\mathsf A_\varphi^c\) it is at least \(\Delta/4\) below \(\tau(P)\). Hence \[\mathcal B_0(\varphi) \ge \frac{\Delta^2}{16} \Pr_{\Pi_0\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_0\cap\mathsf A_\varphi), \qquad \mathcal B_1(\varphi) \ge \frac{\Delta^2}{16} \Pr_{\Pi_1\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_1\cap\mathsf A_\varphi^c).\] The concentration bounds from step 4 give \[\Pr_{\Pi_0\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_0^c)\le\frac1{16}, \qquad \Pr_{\Pi_1\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_1^c)\le\frac1{16},\] and therefore \[\mathcal B_0(\varphi) \ge \frac{\Delta^2}{16}\left(\mathsf Q_0(\mathsf A_\varphi)-\frac1{16}\right), \qquad \mathcal B_1(\varphi) \ge \frac{\Delta^2}{16}\left(1-\mathsf Q_1(\mathsf A_\varphi)-\frac1{16}\right).\] Using \(\max\{x_0,x_1\}\ge(x_0+x_1)/2\) and the total-variation bound, \[\begin{aligned} \max\{\mathcal B_0(\varphi),\mathcal B_1(\varphi)\} &\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{32} \left(1-\bigl(\mathsf Q_1(\mathsf A_\varphi)-\mathsf Q_0(\mathsf A_\varphi)\bigr)-\frac1{16}-\frac1{16}\right)\\ &\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{32} \left(1-\frac18-\frac1{16}-\frac1{16}\right) = \frac{3\Delta^2}{128}. \end{aligned}\] If \(\theta_1\le\theta_0\), the same calculation is applied with the two branches interchanged. Thus, for every measurable raw rule \(\varphi\), \[\frac{3\Delta^2}{128} \le \max\Bigl\{ \mathcal B_0(\varphi),\mathcal B_1(\varphi) \Bigr\}\] in \(\overline{\mathbb R}_+\).

  6. The loss is bounded by \[0\le \ell_\epsilon(P,z)\le 4\] because both \(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(z)\) and \(\tau(P)\) lie in \([-1,1]\) on the model class, and the loss is \(0\) off the model class. The Poisson retention tails used to transfer raw rules to fixed-sample rules are as follows. For any integer \(\mathfrak n\ge1\) and any Poisson mean \(\lambda\ge32768\,\mathfrak n\), \[\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)<\mathfrak n\} \le \exp(-\lambda/8) \le \frac1{4096\,\mathfrak n}.\] Indeed, with \(z_\lambda=\lambda/8\), \[\sqrt{2\lambda z_\lambda}=\lambda/2,\] and \(\{\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)<\mathfrak n\}\subseteq \{\lambda-\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)>\lambda/2\}\); the Poisson Bernstein lower-tail bound gives the exponential term, and \(\exp(-z_\lambda)\le 1/z_\lambda\le1/(4096\mathfrak n)\).

    On the event \(s_+(P)\ge1/2\), the labeled Poisson mean satisfies \[u\,s_+(P)\ge 32768\,n,\] so the labeled retention failure probability is at most \(1/(4096n)\). Similarly, \[v\,s_+(P)\ge 32768\,(n+m),\] and the event of retaining fewer than \(m\) auxiliary observations is contained in the event of retaining fewer than \(n+m\) observations; hence the auxiliary retention failure probability is at most \[\frac1{4096(n+m)}\le \frac1{4096n}.\]

  7. We next transfer the raw fuzzy lower bound to the fixed experiment by the prefix coupling. Fix any measurable fixed-sample decision rule \(\psi\). Define its transferred raw rule \(\varphi_\psi\) as follows. Given an ordered raw output \(y_{\rm raw}\), set \[\varphi_\psi(y_{\rm raw}) = \begin{cases} \psi\bigl(\text{the first \(n\) labeled marks and the first \(m\) auxiliary marks in \(y_{\rm raw}\)}\bigr), & \text{if those marks are present},\\ \zeta_\star,& \text{otherwise}. \end{cases}\] The rule uses only the first \(m\) auxiliary marks, while the raw auxiliary Poisson process has intensity \(v\,s_+(P)=65536(n+m)s_+(P)\); the auxiliary tail estimate below therefore bounds the event of having fewer than \(m\) auxiliary marks by the larger-threshold event of having fewer than \(n+m\). Here \(\zeta_\star\in\mathbb R\) is any fixed real-valued action. For a law \(P\), let \[\mathsf F_P := \bigl\{\text{the ordered raw draw contains fewer than \(n\) labeled marks or fewer than \(m\) auxiliary marks}\bigr\}.\] On \(\mathsf F_P^c\), the first \(n\) labeled marks and first \(m\) auxiliary marks have exactly the fixed-sample law \(\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)\), because the ordered raw experiment is generated by independent Poisson samples from the same labeled and auxiliary mark kernels and the fixed experiment keeps the first prescribed counts.

    For branch \(b\), the exceptional probability under the branch prior predictive is bounded by integrating the conditional failure probability. Splitting according to the scale event gives \[\begin{aligned} \Pr_{\Pi_b\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf F_P) &\le \Pi_b\{P:s_+(P)<1/2\} \\ &\quad+ \int_{\{P:s_+(P)\ge1/2\}} \left[ \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u s_+(P))<n\} + \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(v s_+(P))<m\} \right]d\Pi_b(P). \end{aligned}\] The scale-tail bound from step 4 and the Poisson retention bounds from step 6 therefore yield, for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\), \[\Pr_{\Pi_b\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf F_P) \le \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n}.\] Since \(0\le\ell_\epsilon\le4\), the Bayes risk of the transferred raw rule satisfies \[\begin{aligned} \mathcal B_b(\varphi_\psi) &\le \int \int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y)\,d\Pi_b(P)\\ &\quad+ 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right)\\ &\le \sup_{P}\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y) + 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right). \end{aligned}\] Combining this with the raw fuzzy bound above gives, for every measurable \(\psi\), \[\frac{3\Delta^2}{128} \le \sup_{P}\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y) + 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right).\] Equivalently, \[\frac{3\Delta^2}{128} - 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right) \le \sup_{P}\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y).\] Taking the infimum over \(\psi\) gives \[\frac{3\Delta^2}{128} - 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right) \le \mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.\] The floor inequality from step 3 also gives \(\alpha_{\rm floor}/n\le\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}\), so \[\max\left\{ \frac{3\Delta^2}{128} - 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right), \, \frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n} \right\} \le \mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.\] For the reverse comparison to step 3, start with any measurable estimator \(T\) in Definition 11 and use the same function as the fixed-sample decision rule \(\psi_T\). On \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), clipping cannot increase squared error because \(\tau(P)\in[-1,1]\), while off the model class the class-gated loss is zero. Hence the worst-case decision risk of \(\psi_T\) is no larger than the worst-case mean-squared risk of \(T\). Taking the infimum over \(T\) gives \[\mathfrak R_{\rm dec} \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),\] and consequently \[\max\left\{ \frac{3\Delta^2}{128} - 4\left( \frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n} \right), \, \frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n} \right\} \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\]

  8. It remains only to read off the deterministic arithmetic. From the floor bound, \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}>0.\]

    First suppose \[\frac1n\le \Delta^2.\] Then \[4\left(\frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n}\right) \le 4\left(\frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac{2\Delta^2}{4096}\right) = \frac{9\Delta^2}{4096}.\] Therefore \[\frac{3\Delta^2}{128} - 4\left(\frac{\Delta^2}{16384} +\frac1{4096n} +\frac1{4096n}\right) \ge \frac{87\Delta^2}{4096} \ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.\] The first branch of the maximum in the preceding display gives \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.\]

    Now suppose \[\Delta^2<\frac1n.\] Since \(\alpha_{\rm floor}=1/100\), \[\frac{\Delta^2}{65536} < \frac1{65536\,n} \le \frac1{100\,n} = \frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}.\] The second branch of the maximum gives the same conclusion. Combining the two cases, \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.\]

∎

Lemma 20 converts the raw recipe separation into a lower bound for \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) under the fixed annotation sample sizes. The total-variation requirement supplies the testing affinity, and the variance-scale requirement keeps the normalization fluctuations small relative to the squared raw gap.

The Poisson construction is then converted to a general fixed-size annotation experiment. The transfer statement tracks the raw normalization explicitly, so the resulting lower bound applies to the minimax risk in Definition 11.

Lemma 21 [lem:fixed-sample-common-marginal-transfer] (Fixed-sample transfer).

There exist numerical constants \(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}>0\) and \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}>0\) such that the following holds. For every \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) and every \(C_\epsilon>0\), there is a constant \(c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}>0\) such that, for every common-marginal calibration at overlap level \(\epsilon\) with positive calibration constants and dual gap \(\delta_\epsilon\le 1/4\), every \(\rho\in\mathbb R\), every \(n,m,d,L,k\in\mathbb N\), every \(B,a\in\mathbb R\), and every common-marginal recipe \(R\) at these indices, the implication below holds.

Write \(G_R\) for the recipe’s rare-cell generator measure and \(\theta_{R,b}(w)\) for its raw target on branch \(b\). Let \[\Delta = \int \theta_{R,1}(w)\,dG_R(w) - \int \theta_{R,0}(w)\,dG_R(w).\] Assume:

  • (Sample and dimension.) \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 2\).

  • (Witness data.) The recipe carries a common-marginal Poisson-prior witness at labeled and auxiliary intensities \[u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n, \qquad v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m).\] Equivalently, the witness supplies \[(u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L, \qquad B>0, \qquad a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2, \qquad ka\le \delta_\epsilon,\] identifies the recipe’s labeled and auxiliary intensities with \(u\) and \(v\), and satisfies the corresponding common-marginal bounds at dual gap \(\delta_\epsilon\), variance-bound constant \(C_\epsilon\), and likelihood-decay parameter \(\rho\).

  • (Testing distance.) The raw prior-predictive mixture laws \(M_0\) and \(M_1\) generated by the two branches of the recipe satisfy \[\operatorname{TV}(M_0,M_1)\le \frac18 .\]

  • (Concentration scale.) The realized scale obeys \[kBa\le c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}\Delta^2 .\]

Then the minimax risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) from Definition 11 satisfies \[c_{\mathrm{risk}}\Delta^2 \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 21.

Take \[C_{\mathrm{fixed}}=65536, \qquad c_{\mathrm{risk}}=\frac1{65536}.\] Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) and a positive variance-bound constant \(C_\epsilon\), and set \[c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}:=\frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon}>0.\] Let a calibration, a recipe, and all parameters satisfy the hypotheses of the statement. Put \[\bar\theta_b:=\int \theta_b(w)\,dG(w),\qquad b\in\{0,1\},\] so that the raw-center separation in the statement is \[\Delta=\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0.\] The recipe’s raw prior centers are exactly these two numbers: \[\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0 = \int \theta_1(w)\,dG(w)-\int \theta_0(w)\,dG(w).\] The Poisson-prior witness in the hypotheses identifies the recipe’s labeled and auxiliary intensities as \[u=65536\,n, \qquad v=65536\,(n+m).\] Separately, the concentration-scale hypothesis, the definition of \(c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}\), and \(\Delta=\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0\) give \[kBa \le c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}\Delta^2 = \frac{\Delta^2}{65536\,C_\epsilon} = \frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon} \left|\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0\right|^2.\] Together with \(n\ge1\), \(d\ge2\), and \(\operatorname{TV}(M_0,M_1)\le1/8\), these are precisely the hypotheses of Lemma 20, applied with the present calibration, recipe, variance-bound constant \(C_\epsilon\), likelihood-decay parameter \(\rho\), and intensities \(u=65536n\), \(v=65536(n+m)\). That result yields, for the minimax risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) of Definition 11, \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \ge \frac{1}{65536} \left|\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0\right|^2.\] Since \(|\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0|^2=\Delta^2\) and \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}=1/65536\), this is exactly \[c_{\mathrm{risk}}\Delta^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\]

∎

Lemma 21 is the bridge from a Poisson fuzzy-hypothesis calculation to the fixed-\(n,m\) risk. The testing-distance condition supplies indistinguishability, the concentration-scale condition controls the random raw normalization, and the conclusion converts the raw-center separation \(\Delta\) into a lower bound for \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\).

A separate one-dimensional ingredient records the unavoidable parametric contribution of the labeled sample. It is used together with the rare-cell construction to cover regimes in which the lower-bound rate is governed by outcome labels.

Lemma 22 [lem:parametric-label-floor] (Parametric label floor).

Let \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) be the minimax risk in Definition 11. There exists a universal constant \(c>0\) such that, whenever

  • (Overlap level.) \(0<\epsilon<1/2\),

  • (Labeled sample size.) \(n\ge 1\),

  • (Auxiliary sample size.) \(m\) is a nonnegative integer,

  • (Alphabet size.) \(d\ge 2\),

the minimax risk satisfies \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{c}{n}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 22.

Set \(c=1/100\). Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\). Define \[\eta_n:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n}.\] Then \(\eta_n\ge0\), and \[\eta_n^2=\frac{4}{25n}.\] Consider two laws \(P_{\mathrm{null}}\) and \(P_{\mathrm{pert}}\) in \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) with uniform covariate distribution on \([d]\), propensity \(e_x=1/2\) in every cell, control regression \(1/2\) in every cell, and treated regression respectively \(1/2\) and \(1/2+\eta_n\). Since \(n\ge1\), \(\eta_n\le2/5\), hence \(1/2+\eta_n\le1\). The overlap condition follows from \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), so both laws belong to \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\).

For the labeled samples, the parametric total-variation bound applies once \[n\cdot \frac{(1/2)\eta_n^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)}\le \log 2 .\] Using the displayed value of \(\eta_n^2\), the left side is \(8/25\), and \(8/25\le \log 2\). Therefore \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n}, P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\right)\le \frac12 .\]

The auxiliary treatment-covariate marginals of the two laws are equal: \[(P_{\mathrm{null}})_{XA}=(P_{\mathrm{pert}})_{XA}.\] Indeed, both laws have the same uniform covariate distribution and the same propensity \(e_x=1/2\) in every cell; summing over the binary outcome coordinate gives the same mass for each \((x,a)\). Hence their \(m\)-fold auxiliary product laws are identical. The product total-variation inequality gives \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left( P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_{\mathrm{null}})_{XA}^{\otimes m}, P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_{\mathrm{pert}})_{XA}^{\otimes m} \right) \le \operatorname{TV}\!\left(P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n},P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\right)+0 \le \frac12 .\]

The two average treatment effects are \[\tau(P_{\mathrm{null}})=0, \qquad \tau(P_{\mathrm{pert}})=\eta_n.\] The first identity follows because the two outcome regressions coincide in every cell, and the second because every cell has treated-minus-control contrast \(\eta_n\) and the cell masses sum to one. Thus, with \(s_n:=\eta_n/2\), \[2s_n=\eta_n \le \bigl|\tau(P_{\mathrm{null}})-\tau(P_{\mathrm{pert}})\bigr|.\]

We now record the two-point risk consequence used for Definition 11. Let \(T\in\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\) be any estimator, and let \[\mathsf A_0:=\{z:s_n\le |T(z)-\tau(P_{\mathrm{null}})|\}, \qquad \mathsf A_1:=\{z:s_n\le |T(z)-\tau(P_{\mathrm{pert}})|\}.\] The testing bound at separation \(2s_n\), together with the preceding total-variation bound, yields \[\max\Bigl\{ \Pr_{P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(P_{\mathrm{null}})_{XA}^{\otimes m}}(\mathsf A_0), \Pr_{P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(P_{\mathrm{pert}})_{XA}^{\otimes m}}(\mathsf A_1) \Bigr\} \ge \frac14 .\] Moreover, by the elementary inequality \[s_n^2\,\Pr\{s_n\le |T-\theta|\} \le \mathbb E[(T-\theta)^2],\] applied under each of the two laws, the larger of the two mean-squared risks is at least \(s_n^2/4\). Taking the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) and then the infimum over \(T\in\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\) gives \[\frac{s_n^2}{4}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\]

Finally, \[\frac{s_n^2}{4} = \frac{(\eta_n/2)^2}{4} = \frac{1}{100\,n}.\] Consequently \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge 1/(100n)\), which proves the claim with the universal constant \(c=1/100\).

∎

Lemma 22 fixes the labeled-outcome scale uniformly over auxiliary sample sizes and alphabet sizes. This lower bound matches the \(1/n\) component in Definition 12, so the remaining common-marginal construction can focus on the alphabet-dependent contribution.

One final finite-product identity records how independent coordinates can be embedded into a larger product experiment while adding a distinguished coordinate and deterministic padding. This bookkeeping is useful when the rare-cell construction is placed inside a larger finite alphabet with reservoir coordinates.

Lemma 23 [lem:finite-product-padding-identity] (Finite product padding identity).

Let \(I\) and \(J\) be finite index sets, and let \(\mathsf Y\) be a measurable space whose singletons are measurable. Suppose the following conditions hold:

  • (Embedding.) \(f:I\to J\) is injective.

  • (Distinguished coordinate.) \(r\in J\) satisfies \(r\ne f(i)\) for every \(i\in I\).

  • (Coordinate laws.) \(\mu_i\), \(i\in I\), and \(\nu\) are probability measures on \(\mathsf Y\), and \(y_0\in\mathsf Y\).

Define \(\mathsf A:\mathsf Y^I\times\mathsf Y\to\mathsf Y^J\) by \[\mathsf A(z,y)(j)= \begin{cases} y, & j=r,\\ z_i, & j=f(i)\ \text{for the unique } i\in I,\\ y_0, & j\in J\setminus\bigl(\{r\}\cup f(I)\bigr). \end{cases}\] Define the coordinate laws \(\mathsf H_j\), \(j\in J\), by \[\mathsf H_j= \begin{cases} \nu, & j=r,\\ \mu_i, & j=f(i)\ \text{for the unique } i\in I,\\ \delta_{y_0}, & j\in J\setminus\bigl(\{r\}\cup f(I)\bigr). \end{cases}\] Then \[\mathsf A_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mu_i\right)\otimes\nu\right) = \bigotimes_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 23.

  1. Write \[\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}} :=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mu_i\right)\otimes \nu \quad\text{on }\mathsf Y^I\times\mathsf Y, \qquad \Lambda_{\mathrm{out}} :=\bigotimes_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j \quad\text{on }\mathsf Y^J .\] The map \(\mathsf A\) is measurable. Indeed, for each \(j\in J\), the \(j\)-th coordinate of \(\mathsf A(z,y)\) is the second projection if \(j=r\), is the \(i\)-th coordinate projection from \(z\) if \(j=f(i)\), and is the constant \(y_0\) on the remaining coordinates. The condition \(r\ne f(i)\) separates the distinguished coordinate from the embedded ones, and injectivity of \(f\) makes the embedded coordinate \(i\) unique. Since \(I\) and \(J\) are finite, coordinatewise measurability gives measurability of \(\mathsf A\). It is therefore enough, by uniqueness of finite product measures on measurable rectangles, to prove that for every family of measurable sets \[(\mathsf G_j)_{j\in J}, \qquad \mathsf G_j\in\mathcal Y ,\] one has \[\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\!\left(\mathsf A^{-1} \{w\in\mathsf Y^J:\forall j\in J,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}\right) = \prod_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j).\]

  2. First suppose that there is an unused coordinate \(j_\circ\in J\) such that \[j_\circ\ne r,\qquad j_\circ\ne f(i)\ \text{for every }i\in I, \qquad y_0\notin \mathsf G_{j_\circ}.\] For every \((z,y)\in\mathsf Y^I\times\mathsf Y\), the definition of \(\mathsf A\) gives \[\mathsf A(z,y)_{j_\circ}=y_0.\] Thus \[\mathsf A^{-1}\{w:\forall j,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}=\varnothing,\] so the left-hand side is \(0\). On the right-hand side, the same coordinate has \[\mathsf H_{j_\circ}=\delta_{y_0}, \qquad \mathsf H_{j_\circ}(\mathsf G_{j_\circ}) =\delta_{y_0}(\mathsf G_{j_\circ})=0,\] because \(y_0\notin\mathsf G_{j_\circ}\). Hence \[\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j)=0,\] and the required rectangle identity holds in this case.

  3. It remains to consider the complementary case: \[\forall j\in J,\quad j\ne r\ \text{and}\ \bigl(\forall i\in I,\ j\ne f(i)\bigr) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad y_0\in\mathsf G_j .\] Then the preimage of the rectangle is exactly \[\mathsf A^{-1}\{w:\forall j,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\} = \{z\in\mathsf Y^I:\forall i\in I,\ z_i\in\mathsf G_{f(i)}\} \times \mathsf G_r .\] For the forward inclusion, membership in the rectangle gives the \(r\)-coordinate condition \(y\in\mathsf G_r\), and for each \(i\in I\) it gives \[z_i=\mathsf A(z,y)_{f(i)}\in\mathsf G_{f(i)},\] using \(r\ne f(i)\) and injectivity of \(f\). Conversely, if \(z_i\in\mathsf G_{f(i)}\) for all \(i\) and \(y\in\mathsf G_r\), then each coordinate \(j\in J\) is checked by the three alternatives in the definition of \(\mathsf A\): the coordinate \(r\) gives \(y\), a coordinate \(f(i)\) gives \(z_i\), and an unused coordinate gives \(y_0\in\mathsf G_j\) by the displayed complementary condition.

  4. Using the preceding preimage identity and the finite product formula, \[\begin{aligned} \Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\!\left(\mathsf A^{-1} \{w:\forall j,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}\right) &= \left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mu_i\right) \{z:\forall i,\ z_i\in\mathsf G_{f(i)}\}\, \nu(\mathsf G_r) \\ &= \left(\prod_{i\in I}\mu_i(\mathsf G_{f(i)})\right)\nu(\mathsf G_r). \end{aligned}\] The product over \(J\) has the same value. The distinguished coordinate contributes \[\mathsf H_r(\mathsf G_r)=\nu(\mathsf G_r),\] each embedded coordinate contributes, for \(i\in I\), \[\mathsf H_{f(i)}(\mathsf G_{f(i)})=\mu_i(\mathsf G_{f(i)}),\] again by \(r\ne f(i)\) and injectivity of \(f\), and every unused coordinate \(j\) contributes \[\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j)=\delta_{y_0}(\mathsf G_j)=1\] by the complementary condition. Since \(f\) is injective, the embedded coordinates are indexed without repetition, and therefore \[\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j) = \nu(\mathsf G_r)\prod_{i\in I}\mu_i(\mathsf G_{f(i)}).\] This agrees with the preceding expression for the pushforward on every measurable rectangle, so \[\mathsf A_\#\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{out}},\] which is the desired identity.

∎

Lemma 23 formalizes the product-measure preservation behind this padding step. Injectivity keeps the rare coordinates distinct, the distinguished coordinate carries the added law, and the remaining coordinates are fixed at point masses, so the assembled law is the intended product over the larger index set.

The final calibration statements choose constants so that the rare-cell prior works uniformly across the intensity range needed in Theorem 3. The first gives a ready-to-use recipe with explicit counting, variance, and total-variation controls; the second packages these ingredients into the common-marginal certificate family used by the main converse.

Lemma 24 [lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity] (Uniform intensity common marginal).

For every overlap level \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there are positive constants \[r_\epsilon,\quad D_\epsilon,\quad b_{0,\epsilon},\quad b_\epsilon,\quad \gamma_\epsilon,\quad q_\epsilon,\quad c_\epsilon,\quad \delta_\epsilon,\quad C_\epsilon\] and a number \(0<\rho_\epsilon<1\), with \(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\), such that the following calibration facts hold:

  • (Bandwidth slack.) \(131072\,b_{0,\epsilon}\le b_\epsilon\).

  • (Degree floor.) \(2\le D_\epsilon\).

  • (Rare-cell scale.) \[q_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}\le c_\epsilon, \qquad q_\epsilon= \frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}}, \qquad 4\le q_\epsilon .\]

  • (Dimension scale.) \[\delta_\epsilon r_\epsilon\le \frac1{65536}, \qquad \delta_\epsilon\le \frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16}, \qquad \delta_\epsilon\le \left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}} {2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2 .\]

  • (Variance and counting calibration.) \[8(D_\epsilon+1)b_{0,\epsilon} \le \frac{c_\epsilon^3}{65536\,C_\epsilon},\] \[\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)} {(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon} \le q_\epsilon,\] and \[\left\{ \frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2} {(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon} \right\}^2 \le r_\epsilon .\]

  • (Mixture decay.) For every integer \(n\ge1\), \[C_\epsilon(65536n)c_\epsilon \rho_\epsilon^{\lceil D_\epsilon\log(en)\rceil} \le \frac18 .\]

For every \(n,m,L,d,k\) and every \(u,v,B,a\), if

  • \(n\ge1\), \(L\ge2\), and \(d\ge2\);

  • \(u\ge0\), \(v\ge0\), and \(u+v>0\);

  • \(B>0\), \((u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L\), and \[a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2};\]

  • \(ka\le c_\epsilon\) and \(k+1\le d\),

then there is a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with \(k\) rare cells at scale \((a,B,L)\) whose labeled and auxiliary Poisson intensities are \(u\) and \(v\). Its null and alternative priors are the two branch priors of the recipe, and they satisfy:

  • the recipe is in the finite-atom construction regime, and both priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\);

  • the induced random treatment-covariate tables \(P_{XA}\) have exactly the same law under the two priors;

  • the raw total mass has mean one;

  • the raw target centers obey \[c_\epsilon ka \le \left| \theta_1-\theta_0 \right|,\] where \(\theta_1\) and \(\theta_0\) are the alternative and null raw prior centers;

  • the raw total-mass variance is at most \[C_\epsilon kBa,\] and the larger of the two raw target variances is at most \[C_\epsilon kBa;\]

  • in the raw Poisson experiment with labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\), the two prior-predictive mixture laws have total variation distance at most \[C_\epsilon uka\,\rho_\epsilon^L .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 24.

Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).

  1. By Lemma 17, with \[\kappa_\epsilon:=\frac{1-2\epsilon}{\epsilon}>0,\] there are \(\gamma_\epsilon>0\) and \(\bar c_\epsilon>0\) such that every \(L\ge2\) and \(B>0\), with \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2\), admit a finite signed certificate on \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), annihilating monomials through degree \(L\), whose signed integral of \(p/(p+a)\) is at least \(\bar c_\epsilon\). The one-coordinate marked-Poisson moment-matching estimate supplies constants \(\bar b_\epsilon>0\), \(\bar C_\epsilon>0\), and \(\rho_\epsilon\in(0,1)\) with the following uniform property for the complete normalized certificate object that generates the coordinate mixtures. For every finite index set \(\mathcal I\) and every normalized finite signed moment certificate \[\mathfrak C=\{(p_\iota,\omega_\iota):\iota\in\mathcal I\}\] of order \(L\), meaning that its signed measure \(\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal I}\omega_\iota\delta_{p_\iota}\) has normalized variation \(\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal I}|\omega_\iota|=1\) and the certificate data include the moment equations through degree \(L\), if its variation measure is supported on \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\) and the support scale satisfies \((u+v)B\le \bar b_\epsilon L\), then the two branch marked-Poisson predictive laws \(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1\) associated with that same certificate obey \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1) \le \bar C_\epsilon\,u\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .\] Set \[C_\epsilon:=\max\{\bar C_\epsilon,1\},\qquad c_\epsilon:=\min\{\bar c_\epsilon,1/4\},\qquad b_\epsilon:=\bar b_\epsilon .\] Let \[D_\epsilon :=\max\left\{ 2,\, \frac{\max\{1,\log(8C_\epsilon 65536\,c_\epsilon)+2\}} {-\log\rho_\epsilon} \right\}, \qquad s_\epsilon:=\frac1{65536\,C_\epsilon}.\] Define \[b_{0,\epsilon} := \min\left\{ \frac{b_\epsilon}{131072},\, \frac{c_\epsilon}{8\gamma_\epsilon},\, \frac{s_\epsilon c_\epsilon^3}{8(D_\epsilon+1)} \right\},\] \[q_\epsilon:=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}}, \qquad r_\epsilon := \left( \max\left\{ 1,\, \frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{s_\epsilon c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon} \right\} \right)^2,\] and \[\delta_\epsilon := \min\left\{ \frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16},\, \left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}} {2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2,\, \frac{1}{65536\,r_\epsilon} \right\}.\] All these constants are positive, \(0<\rho_\epsilon<1\), and \(c_\epsilon\le1/4\).
