The first five displayed calibrations follow immediately from the three minima and the definitions: \(131072\,b_{0,\epsilon}\le b_\epsilon\), \(D_\epsilon\ge2\), \[q_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon} =\frac{c_\epsilon}{2}\le c_\epsilon,\qquad q_\epsilon=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}},\qquad q_\epsilon\ge4,\] and \[\delta_\epsilon r_\epsilon\le\frac1{65536},\qquad \delta_\epsilon\le\frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16},\qquad \delta_\epsilon\le \left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}} {2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2 .\] Moreover, \[8(D_\epsilon+1)b_{0,\epsilon} \le \frac{c_\epsilon^3}{65536\,C_\epsilon},\] and the same bound on \(b_{0,\epsilon}\) gives \[\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)} {(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon} \le q_\epsilon .\] The definition of \(r_\epsilon\) gives \[\left\{ \frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2} {(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon} \right\}^2 \le r_\epsilon .\] Finally, put \(\lambda_\epsilon:=-\log\rho_\epsilon>0\) and \(M_\epsilon:=8C_\epsilon 65536\,c_\epsilon\). Since \[\lambda_\epsilon D_\epsilon \ge \max\{1,\log M_\epsilon+2\},\] for every \(n\ge1\), \[\rho_\epsilon^{\lceil D_\epsilon\log(en)\rceil} \le \frac1{M_\epsilon n}.\] Thus \[C_\epsilon(65536n)c_\epsilon \rho_\epsilon^{\lceil D_\epsilon\log(en)\rceil} \le \frac{C_\epsilon 65536\,n\,c_\epsilon}{8C_\epsilon 65536\,c_\epsilon\,n} =\frac18 .\]

  2. Now fix \(n,m,L,d,k,u,v,B,a\) satisfying the hypotheses in the statement. By the bandwidth slack, \[(u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L=\bar b_\epsilon L,\] and \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2>0\). Applying Lemma 17 at this \(L,B\) gives distinct points \(p_j\in[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\) and weights \(\omega_j\) with \[\sum_j|\omega_j|=1,\qquad \sum_j \omega_j p_j^\ell=0\quad(0\le \ell\le L), \qquad c_\epsilon\le \sum_j\omega_j\frac{p_j}{p_j+a}.\] Let \[\sigma:=\sum_j\omega_j\delta_{p_j}, \qquad |\sigma|:=\sum_j|\omega_j|\delta_{p_j},\] and define the polar sign on all \(p\in\mathbb R\) by \[h(p):=\sum_j {\bf 1}_{\{p=p_j\}}\operatorname{sgn}(\omega_j).\] Then \(|\sigma|\) is a probability measure, \(|h(p)|\le1\), and \[\sigma=h\,|\sigma|,\qquad c_\epsilon\le \int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p).\] Also, every certificate node satisfies \(p_j\ge a/\kappa_\epsilon>0\), so \(0\) is not one of the nodes. Hence the displayed definition gives \(h(0)=0\). Define the zero-inflated scalar law \[\nu(E) := \int_E \frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p) + \left(1-\int \frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p)\right){\bf 1}_E(0).\] Since \(p\ge a/\kappa_\epsilon>0\) on the support of \(|\sigma|\), \[0\le \frac{a}{p+a}\le 1 \quad |\sigma|\text{-a.e.}\] and \(|\sigma|\) has total mass one. Hence \[0\le \int \frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p)\le 1,\] so the coefficient of \(\delta_0\) is nonnegative and \(\nu\) is a probability measure supported on \(\{0\}\cup[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\). Its mean \(\bar p:=\int p\,d\nu(p)\) is computed directly from the definition of \(\nu\): the atom at zero contributes nothing, and therefore \[\bar p = \int p\frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p) = a\int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p).\] Because \(0\le p/(p+a)\le1\) on the support, this gives \[0\le\bar p\le a .\] Let \(\mathsf G:=\nu^{\otimes k}\). For every vector \(\boldsymbol\xi\in\mathbb R^k\), set \[\widehat\xi_i := \begin{cases} \xi_i,& \xi_i\in\{p_j\}_j,\\ 0,& \xi_i\notin\{p_j\}_j . \end{cases}\] Under \(\mathsf G\), \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}=\boldsymbol\xi\) almost surely, and the range of \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}\) is finite. Choose one reservoir cell \(x_\star\in[d]\) and distinct rare cells \(x_1,\ldots,x_k\in[d]\setminus\{x_\star\}\), possible because \(k+1\le d\). Put \[p_\star:=1-k\bar p .\] Since \(k a\le c_\epsilon\le1/4\), one has \(p_\star\ge3/4>0\). Define the raw cell masses and raw total mass by \[\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x) := p_\star{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_\star\}} +\sum_{i=1}^k \widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}, \qquad S(\boldsymbol\xi) := p_\star+\sum_{i=1}^k\widehat\xi_i .\] Then \(S(\boldsymbol\xi)>0\) and \[S(\boldsymbol\xi)=\sum_{x=1}^d\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x).\]

  3. Define the propensity field on all cells by \[e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x) := \begin{cases} 1/2, & x=x_\star,\\ 1/2, & x\ne x_\star\ \text{and}\ \sum_i\widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}=0,\\ \displaystyle \epsilon\, \frac{\sum_i\widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}+a} {\sum_i\widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}}, & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases}\] If the last line applies, the denominator is some nonzero \(\widehat\xi_i\in[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), so \[\epsilon \le \epsilon\left(1+\frac{a}{\widehat\xi_i}\right) \le \epsilon(1+\kappa_\epsilon) =1-\epsilon .\] The other cases give \(e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)=1/2\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\). For branches \(b\in\{0,1\}\), define control outcome means to be zero and treated outcome means by \[\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(0)}(x_i) := \frac{1-h(\widehat\xi_i)}2,\qquad \mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(1)}(x_i) := \frac{1+h(\widehat\xi_i)}2,\] with \(\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x_\star)=0\) and \(\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x)=0\) on unused cells. Since \(|h|\le1\), all outcome means lie in \([0,1]\). Let \(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) be the law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) with cell mass \[p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x):=\frac{\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)}{S(\boldsymbol\xi)},\] propensity \(e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)\), zero control outcome mean, and treated outcome mean \(\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x)\). This is a probability law because each raw mass is nonnegative and \[\sum_{x=1}^d p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x) = \frac{\sum_x\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)}{S(\boldsymbol\xi)}=1 .\] For the reservoir cell, the raw mass is \(p_\star>0\) and the propensity is \(1/2\), which lies in \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\). For an unused cell, the raw mass is zero, so the occupied-cell overlap condition has no constraint; the totalized propensity there is \(1/2\). For a rare cell \(x_i\), if \(\widehat\xi_i=0\) then its raw mass is zero. If \(\widehat\xi_i\ne0\), then \(\widehat\xi_i\in[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), and the calculation above gives \[\epsilon\le e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i) =\epsilon\left(1+\frac a{\widehat\xi_i}\right) \le 1-\epsilon .\] The control outcome means are zero, and the treated outcome means are either zero or of the form \((1\pm h(\widehat\xi_i))/2\), hence lie in \([0,1]\). Thus every occupied cell satisfies the overlap bounds and all outcome means are legal binary means, so \(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) by Definition 6. Define \[\Pi_b:=(P_b)_\#\mathsf G,\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.\] These are the two branch priors of the recipe, and the finite range of \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}\) places the recipe in the finite-atom construction regime. The labeled and auxiliary intensities stored in the recipe are, by construction, \(u\) and \(v\). Since \(p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)\) and \(e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)\) are independent of \(b\), \[(P\mapsto P_{XA})_\#\Pi_0 = (P\mapsto P_{XA})_\#\Pi_1 .\]

  4. The raw total mass has mean one: \[\int S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi) = p_\star+\sum_{i=1}^k\int \xi_i\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi) = 1-k\bar p+k\bar p = 1 .\] For branch \(b\), the raw target is \[T_b(\boldsymbol\xi) := \sum_{i=1}^k \widehat\xi_i\, \frac{1+(-1)^{1-b}h(\widehat\xi_i)}2 .\] It satisfies the raw version of Definition 8. Indeed, the control outcome means are zero, so \[\tau(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)) =\sum_{x=1}^d \frac{\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)}{S(\boldsymbol\xi)} \mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x).\] Multiplying by \(S(\boldsymbol\xi)\) cancels the normalization. The reservoir and unused cells contribute zero, and the rare-cell terms give \[S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,\tau(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)) = \sum_{i=1}^k \widehat\xi_i\, \frac{1+(-1)^{1-b}h(\widehat\xi_i)}2 =T_b(\boldsymbol\xi).\] Moreover, \[\int \left[ p\,\frac{1+h(p)}2 - p\,\frac{1-h(p)}2 \right]d\nu(p) = \int p h(p)\,d\nu(p).\] The atom of \(\nu\) at zero contributes zero. On the nonzero support, \(d\nu=(a/(p+a))\,d|\sigma|\) and \(d\sigma=h\,d|\sigma|\), so \[\int p h(p)\,d\nu(p) = a\int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p).\] Therefore the raw prior centers \(\theta_b:=\int T_b\,d\mathsf G\) obey \[\theta_1-\theta_0 = k a\int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p).\] The alternation gap gives \[c_\epsilon\le \int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p),\] so the displayed difference is nonnegative and at least \(c_\epsilon k a\). Hence \[c_\epsilon k a\le |\theta_1-\theta_0|.\]

  5. The support and mean bounds for \(\nu\) give \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathsf G}(S) = \operatorname{Var}_{\mathsf G}\left(\sum_{i=1}^k\xi_i\right) \le \sum_{i=1}^k\mathbb E_\nu[p^2] \le kB\,\mathbb E_\nu[p] \le kBa \le C_\epsilon kBa .\] For each branch define \[g_b(p):= p\,\frac{1+(-1)^{1-b}h(p)}2 .\] Then \(0\le g_b(p)\le p\le B\) on the support of \(\nu\), and \[T_b(\boldsymbol\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^k g_b(\xi_i) \quad\text{almost surely under }\mathsf G .\] Consequently, \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathsf G}(T_b) \le \sum_{i=1}^k\mathbb E_\nu[g_b(p)^2] \le kB\,\mathbb E_\nu[g_b(p)] \le kB\,\mathbb E_\nu[p] \le kBa \le C_\epsilon kBa .\] Taking the maximum over \(b=0,1\) gives the asserted raw target-variance bound. The same bounded-support estimates also give the required square integrability of \(S\) and of both \(T_b\).

  6. It remains to bound the prior-predictive distance in the raw Poisson experiment. For a single rare cell, let \(\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}\) be the finite product space consisting of the two labeled treated-outcome counts, the auxiliary treated count, and the labeled and auxiliary aggregate control counts, and let \(z_{\varnothing}\) be its all-zero element. For \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and \(p\in\mathbb R\), define the raw rare-cell kernel by \[\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p} := \begin{cases} \delta_{z_{\varnothing}}, & p=0,\\ \mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{b,p}, & p\ne0, \end{cases} \qquad \alpha_{u,v}:=\frac{u}{u+v}\in[0,1],\] where \(\mathsf M_{b,p}\) is the branch-\(b\) marked-Poisson law associated with the certificate at latent mass \(p\), and \(\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\) is the fixed splitting kernel that separates the marked treated counts into labeled and auxiliary pieces and carries along the corresponding control-count components. Let \[\mathsf Q_b:=\int \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}\,d\nu(p), \qquad \widetilde{\mathsf Q}_b :=\int \mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{b,p}\,d\nu(p).\] For every measurable event \(E\subseteq\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}\) and every \(p\in\mathbb R\), \[\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{1,p}(E)-\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{0,p}(E) = (\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{1,p})(E) -(\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{0,p})(E).\] Indeed, this is the definition when \(p\ne0\). When \(p=0\), the left side is zero because both raw kernels are \(\delta_{z_{\varnothing}}\). The right side is also zero: as shown above, \(0\) is not a certificate node, so \(h(0)=0\), and the two branch marked-Poisson laws agree at the zero fiber before the fixed splitting is applied. Integrating the displayed identity with respect to \(\nu\) gives, for every measurable \(E\), \[(\mathsf Q_1-\mathsf Q_0)(E) = (\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_1-\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_0)(E).\] Thus the signed finite measures \(\mathsf Q_1-\mathsf Q_0\) and \(\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_1-\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_0\) are equal, and their total variations are equal. Hence \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1) = \operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_0,\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_1).\] Data processing under the splitting kernel and the one-coordinate marked-Poisson estimate give \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1) \le \bar C_\epsilon\,u\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .\] The certificate used here is precisely the finite-node certificate constructed above: its node set is finite, its signed weights have total variation one, all nodes lie in \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), and its moments through degree \(L\) vanish. Thus the normalized finite-certificate and support hypotheses of the one-coordinate estimate are all discharged by the displayed construction, rather than by an additional regularity convention. Independence of the \(k\) rare coordinates yields \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(\mathsf Q_0^{\otimes k},\mathsf Q_1^{\otimes k}\right) \le k\,\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1) \le \bar C_\epsilon\,u\,k\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .\] It remains to identify this product experiment with the raw Poisson experiment generated by the constructed laws. Let \(\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) be the conditional raw Poisson law generated by \(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) at raw scale \(S(\boldsymbol\xi)\). Let \[\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}} := \left\{y:\{0,1\}\to(\mathbb N\times\mathbb N)\times\mathbb N\right\}\] be the single-cell raw-count space; \(y(\alpha)=((y^{\mathrm{s}}_\alpha,y^{\mathrm{f}}_\alpha),y^{\mathrm{aux}}_\alpha)\) records labeled successes, labeled failures, and auxiliary counts in arm \(\alpha\). Let \(z_{\mathrm{cell},0}\in\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\) be the all-zero cell observation, and let \[\mathfrak r:\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}\to\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\] send the five rare-cell counts to the two arm-indexed cell observations: in the treated arm it records the treated success count, treated failure count, and auxiliary treated count, while in the control arm it records zero successes, the labeled control count, and the auxiliary control count. The common reservoir law is \[\mathsf Q_\star := \bigotimes_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}} \left \{\operatorname{Poi}(0)\otimes \operatorname{Poi}\left(\frac{u p_\star}{2}\right)\otimes \operatorname{Poi}\left(\frac{v p_\star}{2}\right)\right\}.\] For fixed \(\boldsymbol\xi\), define rare-coordinate cell laws by \[\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i} := (\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i})_\#\mathfrak r, \qquad i=1,\ldots,k,\] and define a zero law \(\mathsf H_0:=\delta_{z_{\mathrm{cell},0}}\). For \(\boldsymbol y=(y_1,\ldots,y_k)\in\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}^k\) and \(y_\star\in\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\), define the deterministic assembly map by \[\bigl[\mathsf A(\boldsymbol y,y_\star)\bigr](x,\alpha) := \begin{cases} y_\star(\alpha), & x=x_\star,\\ y_i(\alpha), & x=x_i\text{ for the unique }i,\\ z_{\mathrm{cell},0}(\alpha), & x\notin\{x_\star,x_1,\ldots,x_k\}. \end{cases}\] The middle line is unambiguous because the rare cells are distinct and none is the reservoir. By Lemma 23, applied with the embedding \(i\mapsto x_i\), distinguished coordinate \(x_\star\), coordinate laws \(\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\), reservoir law \(\mathsf Q_\star\), and filler \(z_{\mathrm{cell},0}\), \[\mathsf A_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right) = \bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x},\] where \[\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x} := \begin{cases} \mathsf Q_\star, & x=x_\star,\\ \mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}, & x=x_i\text{ for the unique }i,\\ \mathsf H_0, & x\notin\{x_\star,x_1,\ldots,x_k\}. \end{cases}\]

    Equivalently, if \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}}\) denotes the map \[\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}}(\boldsymbol z,z_\star) := \mathsf A\bigl((\mathfrak r(z_1),\ldots,\mathfrak r(z_k)),z_\star\bigr),\] then \[(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right) = \bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x},\] because pushforward by \(\mathfrak r\) in each rare coordinate turns \(\bigotimes_i\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\) into \(\bigotimes_i\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\). More explicitly, coordinatewise pushforward gives \[\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\right)_\# \bigl[(z_i)_i\mapsto(\mathfrak r(z_i))_i\bigr] = \bigotimes_{i=1}^k\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}.\] Taking the product with \(\mathsf Q_\star\), then composing this coordinate map with \(\mathsf A\), gives \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}}\). Functoriality of pushforward therefore turns the left side of the padding identity into the asserted assembled rare-coordinate law.

    We now compare these coordinate laws with the conditional raw Poisson law cell by cell. At the reservoir, raw scale multiplication cancels the normalization: \[S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=p_\star, \qquad e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=\frac12, \qquad \mu^{(b)}_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=\mu^{(b)}_{0,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=0.\] Thus each arm has raw treatment-covariate mass \(p_\star/2\), zero labeled-success rate, labeled-failure rate \(u p_\star/2\), and auxiliary rate \(v p_\star/2\). Therefore the reservoir coordinate law of \(\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) is \[\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_\star}=\mathsf Q_\star .\] At a rare cell \(x_i\), put \(p=\widehat\xi_i\). If \(p=0\), then \[S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=0,\] so all labeled and auxiliary Poisson rates at this cell are zero. The conditional raw Poisson coordinate law is \(\delta_{z_{\mathrm{cell},0}}\). The raw rare-cell kernel is also \(\delta_{z_{\varnothing}}\), and \(\mathfrak r(z_{\varnothing})=z_{\mathrm{cell},0}\), hence \[(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,0})_\#\mathfrak r =\delta_{z_{\mathrm{cell},0}} =\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_i} .\] If \(p>0\), define \[s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p):=\epsilon(p+a), \qquad s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p):=p-s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p), \qquad \eta_1:=1, \quad \eta_0:=-1.\] The construction gives \[S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=p, \qquad e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=\frac{s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)}{p}, \qquad \mu^{(b)}_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=\frac{1+\eta_b h(p)}2, \qquad \mu^{(b)}_{0,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=0.\] Consequently the raw treatment-covariate masses in the treated and control arms are \[p\,e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p), \qquad p\{1-e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)\}=s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p).\] The conditional raw Poisson coordinate law at \(x_i\) is therefore the product law on \(\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\) with treated-arm rates \[\frac{u s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)(1+\eta_b h(p))}{2}, \qquad \frac{u s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)(1-\eta_b h(p))}{2}, \qquad v s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)\] for labeled successes, labeled failures, and auxiliary counts, and with control-arm rates \[0, \qquad u s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p), \qquad v s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p)\] for labeled successes, labeled failures, and auxiliary counts. The split marked rare-cell law \(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}\) is the fivefold product law whose coordinates have exactly the nonzero rates just displayed: treated success, treated failure, auxiliary treated, labeled control, and auxiliary control. Applying \(\mathfrak r\) places the first three counts in the treated arm and the last two in the control arm, with zero control successes. Product Poisson laws are determined by their coordinate rates, so \[(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p})_\#\mathfrak r = \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_i} \qquad(p=\widehat\xi_i>0).\] Combining the two rare-cell cases gives \[(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i})_\#\mathfrak r = \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_i} \qquad(1\le i\le k).\] At an unused cell the raw mass is zero, so all rates are zero and its coordinate law is \[\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x}=\mathsf H_0 .\] Thus the coordinate laws of the conditional raw Poisson experiment are precisely the padded product coordinate laws: reservoir law at \(x_\star\), the pushed-forward rare laws at the rare cells, and the zero law elsewhere. Since conditional raw Poisson counts are independent across cells, \[\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi) = \bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x}.\] The preceding coordinate identification, together with the padding identity \[\mathsf A_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right) = \bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x},\] which follows from Lemma 23, yields the pointwise fiber identity \[\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi) = (\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right).\] This equality uses more than the abstract padding identity: the reservoir calculation fixes the distinguished coordinate, the two rare-cell cases identify every pushed-forward rare law, and the unused-cell calculation supplies the Dirac fillers. Independence of the raw Poisson counts then identifies the conditional experiment with the resulting finite product. Hence every coordinate law on the two sides is the same before finite-product uniqueness is invoked. The identity holds for every \(\boldsymbol\xi\). Integrating it against \(\mathsf G\) and using \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}=\boldsymbol\xi\) almost surely gives \[\int \mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi) = (\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left( \left[\int \bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\xi_i}\,d\nu^{\otimes k}(\boldsymbol\xi)\right] \otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right).\] For cylinder events \(E_1\times\cdots\times E_k\subseteq\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}^k\), independence under \(\nu^{\otimes k}\) gives \[\int \prod_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\xi_i}(E_i)\,d\nu^{\otimes k}(\boldsymbol\xi) = \prod_{i=1}^k \int \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}(E_i)\,d\nu(p) = \prod_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q_b(E_i),\] because each map \(p\mapsto\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}(E_i)\) is measurable and takes values in \([0,1]\). Finite Fubini therefore factors the integral of their product under \(\nu^{\otimes k}\) into the product of the coordinate integrals. Both sides define probability measures on the finite product space, and measurable rectangles form a generating \(\pi\)-system; the \(\pi\)-\(\lambda\) uniqueness theorem extends the displayed equality from rectangles to the whole product \(\sigma\)-algebra. Thus \[\int \bigotimes_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\xi_i}\,d\nu^{\otimes k}(\boldsymbol\xi) = \mathsf Q_b^{\otimes k}.\] Consequently the branch- \(b\) raw prior-predictive law is \[\mathsf R_b := \int \mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi) = (\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(\mathsf Q_b^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right).\] Appending the common reservoir law and applying the deterministic assembly map cannot increase total variation, so \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf R_0,\mathsf R_1) \le \operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0^{\otimes k},\mathsf Q_1^{\otimes k}) \le \bar C_\epsilon\,u\,k\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L \le C_\epsilon\,u\,k\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .\] The constructed recipe with priors \(\Pi_0,\Pi_1\) therefore satisfies all displayed calibration, support, common-table, mean, separation, variance, and mixture-distance conclusions.

∎
Lemma 25 [lem:common-marginal-canonical-certificate-family] (Canonical certificate family).

There exist real constants \(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}\) and \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}\) with \[0<C_{\mathrm{fixed}}, \qquad 0<c_{\mathrm{risk}},\] such that, for every overlap level \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there are a common-marginal calibration in the sense of Definition 19 and real constants \[C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon,c_{\mathrm{small}},c_{\mathrm{floor}}\] with the following properties. Write \(b_0\) for the calibration bandwidth constant, \(b_\epsilon\) for the uniform bandwidth constant, \(\gamma_\epsilon\) for the shift constant, \(c_\epsilon\) for the dual-gap constant, and \(C_d\) for the dimension constant supplied by the calibration. These calibration constants are positive, and \[c_\epsilon\le \frac14 .\] The auxiliary constants satisfy \[0<C_\epsilon, \qquad \rho_\epsilon\in(0,1), \qquad 0<c_{\mathrm{small}}, \qquad 0<c_{\mathrm{floor}}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}\le c_{\mathrm{risk}} .\] Moreover:

  • (Fixed-sample transfer.) For every \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), and every common-marginal prior handle \(H\) at \((n,m,d)\) in the sense of Definition 19, let \(L,B,a,k\) denote the degree, bandwidth, shift, and rare-cell count components of \(H\), and let \(\theta_0(H)\) and \(\theta_1(H)\) denote the two raw prior centers of its recipe. If the recipe of \(H\) carries the exact common-marginal Poisson-prior construction at bandwidth constant \(b_0\), information size \(n+m\), labeled intensity \[u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n,\] auxiliary intensity \[v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m),\] and constants \((C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon)\), including the corresponding finite-support prior data, exact common treatment-covariate marginal table, moment-matching identities through degree \(L\), raw-center separation data, and Poisson likelihood-ratio bound at rate \(C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L\), if the two raw prior-predictive Poisson experiment laws supplied by that recipe have total variation distance at most \(1/8\), and if \[kBa \le c_{\mathrm{small}} \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2,\] then \[c_{\mathrm{risk}} \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2 \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),\] where \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) is the minimax risk from Definition 11.

  • (Canonical handles.) For every \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), there exists a common-marginal prior handle \(H\) at \((n,m,d)\). Let \(L,B,a,k\) denote its degree, bandwidth, shift, and rare-cell count components, set \[\Delta_H=c_\epsilon ka, \qquad D(n,m,d)=\min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log(en)^2}\right\},\] and let \(\theta_0(H)\) and \(\theta_1(H)\) denote the two raw prior centers of its recipe. The same constants satisfy \[L\ge2, \qquad 2C_{\mathrm{fixed}}b_0\le b_\epsilon,\] \[\bigl(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n+C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m)\bigr)B \le b_\epsilon L,\] \[\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_0(H)) = \mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_1(H)), \qquad \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] On the finite-atom branch, the handle also satisfies \[ka\le c_\epsilon,\] and its recipe carries the exact common-marginal Poisson-prior construction at bandwidth constant \(b_0\), information size \(n+m\), labeled intensity \(u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n\), auxiliary intensity \(v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m)\), and constants \((C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon)\), including the finite-support prior data, exact common treatment-covariate marginal table, moment-matching identities through degree \(L\), raw-center separation data, and Poisson likelihood-ratio bound at rate \(C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L\). The recipe bounds hold at these intensities: both priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) from Definition 6, they induce the same random \(P_{XA}\) law, their raw-center separation is at least \(c_\epsilon ka\), the raw total mass of the generator has mean one, the raw total mass and raw targets are square-integrable with generator variances at most \(C_\epsilon kBa\), and the raw mixture total variation is at most \(C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L\). Moreover, the two raw prior-predictive Poisson experiment laws supplied by the recipe have total variation distance at most \(1/8\), and \[\Delta_H\le \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|, \qquad kBa\le c_{\mathrm{small}}\Delta_H^2,\] \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}\Delta_H^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d), \qquad C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le \Delta_H^2.\] On the parametric branch, \[C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 25.

Set \[C_{\mathrm{fixed}}=65536, \qquad c_{\mathrm{risk}}=\frac1{65536}.\] These constants are positive. Fix \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\). By Lemma 24, choose a common-marginal calibration and constants \(C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon\) with \[C_\epsilon>0,\qquad \rho_\epsilon\in(0,1),\] together with the uniform construction and closure bounds supplied there. Define \[c_{\mathrm{small}}=\frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}=\frac1{65536}.\] Then \[c_{\mathrm{small}}>0,\qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}>0,\qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}\le c_{\mathrm{risk}},\] and Lemma 24 supplies positive \(b_0,b_\epsilon,\gamma_\epsilon,q_\epsilon,c_\epsilon,C_d\), with \(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\). We retain that statement’s notation: \(q_\epsilon\) is the rare-count coefficient, \(c_\epsilon\) is the dual-gap constant, and \(C_d\) is the dimension constant.

First consider the fixed-sample transfer assertion. Let \(n,m,d,H\) satisfy the hypotheses in that assertion, and write \[\Delta = \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|.\] By the hypotheses of the fixed-sample assertion, the recipe carried by \(H\) comes with an exact Poisson-prior witness at \[u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n, \qquad v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m),\] with constants \(C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon\). The same hypotheses give total variation at most \(1/8\) for its prior-predictive laws, and the assumed variance absorption is \[kBa\le c_{\mathrm{small}}\Delta^2 = \frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon}\Delta^2 .\] Applying the fixed-sample recipe transfer bound Lemma 20 at these intensities gives \[\frac1{65536}\Delta^2 \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Since \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}=1/65536\), this is exactly the fixed-sample transfer conclusion.

It remains to construct the canonical handle for arbitrary \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\). Write \[N=n+m,\qquad \ell_n=\log(en), \qquad D(n,m,d)=\min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}\right\}.\] Let \(r_\epsilon\) denote the calibration regime constant, \(D_\epsilon\) the calibration degree constant, and \(q_\epsilon\) the calibration rare-count constant. The proof splits according to whether the parametric regime holds: \[D(n,m,d)\le \frac{r_\epsilon}{n}.\] In both regimes set \[L=\left\lceil D_\epsilon\ell_n\right\rceil,\qquad B=\frac{b_0L}{N},\qquad a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},\] and \[k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor\right\}.\] The calibration closure in Lemma 24 gives \[L\ge2\] and \[2C_{\mathrm{fixed}}b_0=131072\,b_0\le b_\epsilon .\] Moreover, since \(n\le N\), \[(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n+C_{\mathrm{fixed}}N)B \le (131072\,N)\frac{b_0L}{N} = 131072\,b_0L \le b_\epsilon L.\] Thus the degree and bandwidth conclusions hold for either branch.

Suppose first that \[D(n,m,d)\le \frac{r_\epsilon}{n}.\] The local testing gap used by the parametric handle is admissible because \[\frac{c_\epsilon}{\sqrt n}\le c_\epsilon\le \frac14,\] where \(\sqrt n\ge1\). Let \(H\) be the parametric common-marginal prior handle with the above values of \(L,B,a,k\). Its branch condition makes the finite-atom predicate false in this case. Consequently all conclusions whose hypotheses include the finite-atom predicate are vacuous. The common treatment-covariate marginal identity is the handle identity \[\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_0(H)) = \mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_1(H)).\] The risk floor follows from the parametric two-point floor: \[\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n} = \frac{1/65536}{n} \le \frac{1/100}{n} \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Finally, the calibration closure gives \[C_d\,r_\epsilon\le \frac1{65536}=c_{\mathrm{floor}},\] and hence, using the parametric-regime inequality, \[C_d\,D(n,m,d) \le C_d\,\frac{r_\epsilon}{n} \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}.\] This proves all required canonical-handle properties on the parametric branch.

Now suppose that the parametric regime fails: \[D(n,m,d)>\frac{r_\epsilon}{n}.\] With \(L,B,a,k\) as above, the quantities \(B\) and \(a\) are positive. From \[k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor\right\}\] and the elementary inequality \(\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\rfloor\le q_\epsilon NL\), valid since \(q_\epsilon NL\ge0\), we have \[k\le q_\epsilon NL.\] Therefore \[ka \le q_\epsilon NL\cdot \frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL} = q_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_0 \le c_\epsilon,\] where the last inequality is the rare-scale calibration inequality. Hence the rare shift is small.

Apply the finite-atom part of Lemma 24 with \[u=65536\,n,\qquad v=65536\,N.\] The preceding bandwidth bound, positivity of \(B\), the identity \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2\), the rare-shift bound \(ka\le c_\epsilon\), and \(k+1\le d\) produce a recipe \(R\) at these intensities. The bounds supplied for this recipe include the finite-atom construction flag, support in \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), equality of the induced treatment-covariate marginal table, the raw total-mass mean-one identity, the raw-center separation, the raw total-mass and target variance bounds, and the raw mixture total-variation bound \[\operatorname{TV} \bigl(\mathsf P_{0,R}^{\mathrm{Pois}},\mathsf P_{1,R}^{\mathrm{Pois}}\bigr) \le C_\epsilon u k a \rho_\epsilon^L.\] Together with \(n\ge1\), \(L\ge2\), \(d\ge2\), the nonnegativity of \(u,v\), the positivity of \(u+v\), the bandwidth and shift identities, and the rare-count fit \(k+1\le d\), these same bounds form the exact Poisson-prior witness used below. Let \(H\) be the corresponding handle. Its branch condition identifies this recipe with the finite-atom branch. The common marginal identity again gives \[\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_0(H)) = \mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_1(H)).\] The recipe bounds also give \[\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right| \ge c_\epsilon ka = \Delta_H,\] the raw total mass has mean one, the raw total mass and raw targets are square-integrable with variances at most \(C_\epsilon kBa\), and the raw mixture total variation is bounded by \[C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L.\] The calibration decay inequality and the bound \(ka\le c_\epsilon\) imply \[\operatorname{TV} \bigl(\mathsf P_{0,H}^{\mathrm{Pois}},\mathsf P_{1,H}^{\mathrm{Pois}}\bigr) \le C_\epsilon(65536\,n)ka\rho_\epsilon^L \le C_\epsilon(65536\,n)c_\epsilon\rho_\epsilon^L \le \frac18 .\] Thus the prior-predictive total variation condition required by the transfer rule holds.

It remains to record the two absorptions used by the handle. The counting estimate used in the finite-atom branch is \[\frac{L^2}{c_{\mathrm{small}}c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}\le k .\] Indeed, write \(N=n+m\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\). Since the branch condition is \[D(n,m,d)>\frac{r_\epsilon}{n},\] the minimum in \(D(n,m,d)\) forces \[\frac{r_\epsilon}{n} < \frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}.\] Put \[K=c_{\mathrm{small}}c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon>0.\] The calibration inequality \[\left( \frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K} \right)^2 \le r_\epsilon\] implies \[\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}\le\sqrt{r_\epsilon}.\] Meanwhile, the failed-regime inequality above gives \[\frac{\sqrt{r_\epsilon}\,N\ell_n}{\sqrt n}<d.\] Using the ceiling bound \(L\le(D_\epsilon+1)\ell_n\) and \[\ell_n\sqrt n\le 2n\le 2N,\] we obtain the complete cross-multiplication \[\begin{aligned} \frac{2L^2}{K} &\le \frac{2(D_\epsilon+1)^2\ell_n^2}{K} \\ &= \left(\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}\right) \frac{N\ell_n}{\sqrt n} \frac{\ell_n\sqrt n}{2N} \\ &\le \frac{\sqrt{r_\epsilon}\,N\ell_n}{\sqrt n} <d. \end{aligned}\] Equivalently, the proof has compared the nonnegative quantities through the strict square inequality \[\left(\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}N\ell_n\right)^2 < (d\sqrt n)^2,\] and then divided by the positive factor \(\sqrt n\). Weakening the strict bound gives \[\frac{2L^2}{K} \le d.\] Now split according to the actual comparison defining the minimum in \(k\). If \[d-1\le \left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor,\] then \[k=d-1.\] Since \(d\ge2\), \[\frac d2\le d-1,\] and hence \[\frac{L^2}{K} \le \frac d2\le d-1=k .\] If instead \[\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor<d-1,\] then \[k=\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor .\] The calibration gives \[\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)}{K} \le q_\epsilon,\] and, since \(q_\epsilon NL\ge1\), the elementary floor bound \(\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\rfloor\ge q_\epsilon NL/2\) gives \[k=\bigl\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\bigr\rfloor \ge \frac{q_\epsilon NL}{2} \ge \frac{2(D_\epsilon+1)NL}{K}.\] Using \(L\le(D_\epsilon+1)\ell_n\) and \(\ell_n\le N\), this is at least \[\frac{L^2}{K}.\] Thus the displayed counting estimate holds in both subcases. Recall here that \(c_\epsilon\) denotes the calibration dual-gap constant; the rare-count coefficient is \(q_\epsilon\). Using \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2\), the counting estimate yields \[kBa = \left(\frac{k a^2}{\gamma_\epsilon}\right)L^2 \le \frac{k a^2}{\gamma_\epsilon} \bigl(kc_{\mathrm{small}}c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon\bigr) = c_{\mathrm{small}}(c_\epsilon ka)^2 = c_{\mathrm{small}}\Delta_H^2.\] The recipe separation bound gives \[\Delta_H=c_\epsilon ka \le \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|.\] Consequently \[kBa \le c_{\mathrm{small}} \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2 .\] The fixed-sample transfer already proved above therefore gives \[c_{\mathrm{floor}} \left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2 \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),\] and hence \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}\Delta_H^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] As before, the parametric floor gives \[\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Finally we verify the dimension absorption for the above \(L,k,a\). Since \[a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2} =\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL},\] it is enough to compare \(C_dD(n,m,d)\) with \[\left(c_\epsilon k\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL}\right)^2.\] Split according to which term realizes \[k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor\right\}.\] On the cap-active subcase, \(k=d-1\). Because \(d\ge2\), \[\frac d2\le d-1=k.\] The definition of \(D(n,m,d)\) and the dimension calibration give \[\begin{aligned} C_dD(n,m,d) &\le C_d\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2} \\ &\le \left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2 \frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}. \end{aligned}\] All factors here are nonnegative. The two bounds \(d\le2k\) and \(L\le(D_\epsilon+1)\ell_n\) therefore give, after cross-multiplication by the positive denominators, \[0\le \frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_0d} {2(D_\epsilon+1)N\ell_n} \le c_\epsilon k\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL} =c_\epsilon ka.\] Squaring this nonnegative comparison proves \[C_dD(n,m,d)\le(c_\epsilon ka)^2\] on the cap-active subcase. On the floor-active subcase, \(k=\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\rfloor\). The calibration includes the rare-count identity \[q_\epsilon=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_0}.\] The calibration has \(q_\epsilon\ge4\), while \(N\ge1\) and \(L\ge2\), so \(q_\epsilon NL\ge1\). The elementary floor bound therefore gives \[k\ge \frac{q_\epsilon NL}{2}.\] Thus, with every factor nonnegative, \[ka \ge \frac{q_\epsilon NL}{2}\cdot\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL} =\frac{c_\epsilon}{4}.\] Since \(D(n,m,d)\le1\), the calibration gives \(C_d\le c_\epsilon^4/16\), and squaring preserves the preceding comparison, \[C_dD(n,m,d) \le C_d \le \frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16} =\left(c_\epsilon\frac{c_\epsilon}{4}\right)^2 \le (c_\epsilon ka)^2.\] Thus in both subcases \[C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le (c_\epsilon ka)^2=\Delta_H^2.\] This proves every required canonical-handle property on the finite-atom branch, and completes the construction for all \(n,m,d\).

∎

The constants in Lemma 24 coordinate the degree, bandwidth, rare-cell count, and mixture decay. The shared-law conclusion for the random treatment–covariate tables is the structural feature that makes the prior compatible with auxiliary annotation data, and the final total-variation display supplies the testing bound used by Lemmas 20 and 21. Lemma 25 then assembles the transfer constants, the canonical handle, the parametric label floor, and the finite-atom branch into the certificate invoked by Theorem 3.

One-coordinate marked-Poisson input.

The load-bearing analytic estimate is a one-coordinate marked-Poisson total-variation bound for normalized signed certificates. For a normalized signed certificate supported on \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), with moments through degree \(L\) equal to zero and \((u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L\), its two marked-Poisson predictive laws satisfy \[\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1) \le C_\epsilon u a\rho_\epsilon^L, \qquad 0<\rho_\epsilon<1.\] Expanding each marked-Poisson likelihood in the latent mass gives the intuition behind the bound: moment matching cancels the polynomial terms through degree \(L\), and the bandwidth condition leaves a geometrically controlled exponential-series tail. Tensorization over rare cells contributes the factor \(k\), while the common reservoir and deterministic splitting and assembly maps preserve or contract total variation.

Known-marginal experiment and verification note

The benchmark in Definition 15, Theorem 1, and Theorem 5 uses the same labeled experiment as the annotation problem, but supplies the treatment–covariate marginal as exact side information. This appendix records the corresponding experiment explicitly so that the comparison with the auxiliary-sample experiment in Definition 9 has a fixed statistical object.

Definition 30 [def:known-marginal-experiment] (Known-marginal experiment \(\mathcal E^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d,\epsilon}\)).

For \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), the known-marginal experiment is \[\mathcal E^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d,\epsilon} := \{(P^{\otimes n},P_{XA}):P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\}.\] The first coordinate generates \(\mathcal L_n\), and the second coordinate is the exact \(2d\)-cell table observed as deterministic side information.

⊢ Lean

The deterministic table \(P_{XA}\) contains the full joint distribution of treatment and covariates under the same observed-data law \(P\) used for the labeled sample. Thus the benchmark isolates the contribution of outcome labels after the treatment–covariate frequencies are known exactly. This is the population analogue of having unlimited annotation data for \((X,A)\), and it is the object compared with finite-\(m\) annotation in Theorem 5.

The risk \(R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)\) in Definition 15 takes the infimum over \(\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}\), the estimators measurable with respect to the labeled sample and this exact table. The boundary result in Theorem 1 therefore describes the benchmark rate for the known-marginal problem, while Theorem 5 identifies the annotation regimes in which the finite auxiliary sample attains the same limiting benchmark. The construction is aligned with the finite-alphabet causal model in Definition 6 and the average treatment effect functional in Definition 8; the overlap and sampling conditions remain those stated in Assumptions 1 and 2.

The next statements record the finite side-information comparison underlying that benchmark. The finite-cover statement is the compactness input that turns exact side information into finitely many stable decision rules; the causal specialization then yields convergence of empirical side samples to the exact-side benchmark; and the measurable comparison formalizes exact side information as at least as informative as an empirical side sample.

Lemma 26 [lem:finite-side-cover-approximate-decision] (Finite side cover).

Let \(\Theta\) be a nonempty compact topological space, let \(\mathsf Z\) be a finite set, let \(\mathsf C\) be a finite nonempty set, and let \(l\le u\). Suppose the following conditions hold.

  • (Laws.) For each \(\theta\in\Theta\), \(\Lambda_\theta\) is a probability vector on \(\mathsf Z\) and \(\Xi_\theta\) is a probability vector on \(\mathsf C\).

  • (Target.) For each \(\theta\in\Theta\), \(\upsilon_\theta\in[l,u]\).

  • (Continuity.) For every \(z\in\mathsf Z\) and \(c\in\mathsf C\), the maps \(\theta\mapsto\Lambda_\theta(z)\), \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta(c)\), and \(\theta\mapsto\upsilon_\theta\) are continuous.

For probability vectors \(\nu,\nu'\) on \(\mathsf C\), set \[D_\infty(\nu,\nu')=\max_{c\in\mathsf C}|\nu(c)-\nu'(c)|.\] Define \[\mathfrak R_\star = \inf_{\psi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta} \sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\Xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2,\] where \(\psi\) ranges over all maps \[\psi:\mathsf Z\times\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C)\to[l,u].\] Then for every \(\delta>0\), there exist an integer \(k\ge1\), centers \(\theta^\circ_1,\ldots,\theta^\circ_k\in\Theta\), decisions \(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_k:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\), radii \(\operatorname{rad}_1,\ldots,\operatorname{rad}_k\), and a margin \(\eta>0\) such that \[\eta<\operatorname{rad}_j\] for every \(j\), every \(\theta\in\Theta\) admits some \(j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\) with \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j})<\operatorname{rad}_j-\eta,\] and, for every \(j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\) and every \(\theta\in\Theta\), \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j})<\operatorname{rad}_j \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\beta_j(z)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 \le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 26.

  1. For a decision \(b:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\), write \[R_\theta(b) = \sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z)\bigl(b(z)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] Since \(b(z),\upsilon_\theta\in[l,u]\), each squared term is at most \((u-l)^2\), and since \(\Lambda_\theta\) is a probability vector, \[0\le R_\theta(b)\le (u-l)^2 \qquad\text{for every }\theta\in\Theta .\] In particular the constant decision \(b_0(z)=l\) is admissible.

  2. Fix \(\theta_0\in\Theta\). Let \[\mathcal F(\theta_0) = \{\theta\in\Theta:\Xi_\theta=\Xi_{\theta_0}\}\] and let \[V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0) = \inf_{\beta:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]}\ \sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)} R_\theta(\beta).\] The fiber is nonempty because it contains \(\theta_0\). By the previous bound, the worst-case risk of the constant decision \(b_0\) over this fiber is at most \((u-l)^2\), hence \(V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le (u-l)^2\). Thus the set of all fiber values is bounded above.

    For an exact-side rule \(\psi:\mathsf Z\times\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C)\to[l,u]\), write \[R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi) = \sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\Xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2, \qquad \mathfrak R_\star = \inf_\psi\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi).\] For any such \(\psi\), define the fiber decision \[\beta_{\psi,\theta_0}(z)=\psi(z,\Xi_{\theta_0}).\] If \(\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)\), then \(\Xi_\theta=\Xi_{\theta_0}\), so \[R_\theta(\beta_{\psi,\theta_0})=R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi).\] Consequently \[V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0) \le \sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)}R_\theta(\beta_{\psi,\theta_0}) \le \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi).\] Taking the infimum over \(\psi\) gives \[V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le \mathfrak R_\star, \qquad \sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\vartheta)\le \mathfrak R_\star.\]

    For the reverse inequality, set \[\mathfrak s=\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\vartheta).\] Let \(\mathfrak a\) be any real number with \(\mathfrak s<\mathfrak a\). Then every fiber value is strictly below \(\mathfrak a\). Since the fiber risks are nonnegative, the strict infimum property gives, for each \(\theta_0\), a decision \(\beta_{\theta_0}:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\) such that \[\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)}R_\theta(\beta_{\theta_0})<\mathfrak a.\] Define a representative map on the whole simplex by \[\operatorname{rep}(\nu)= \begin{cases} \text{one }\theta\in\Theta\text{ with }\Xi_\theta=\nu, & \text{if such a }\theta\text{ exists},\\ \text{an arbitrary point of }\Theta, & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases}\] This is defined for every \(\nu\in\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C)\) because \(\Theta\) is nonempty. Now assemble the exact-side rule \[\psi_{\mathfrak a}(z,\nu)=\beta_{\operatorname{rep}(\nu)}(z).\] For each \(\theta\in\Theta\), the representative \(\operatorname{rep}(\Xi_\theta)\) has side law \(\Xi_\theta\), so \(\theta\in\mathcal F(\operatorname{rep}(\Xi_\theta))\). Hence \[R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi_{\mathfrak a}) = R_\theta\bigl(\beta_{\operatorname{rep}(\Xi_\theta)}\bigr) <\mathfrak a.\] Therefore \[\mathfrak R_\star \le \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi_{\mathfrak a}) \le \mathfrak a.\] Since this holds for every \(\mathfrak a>\mathfrak s\), \[\mathfrak R_\star\le\mathfrak s.\] Combining the two inequalities yields \[\mathfrak R_\star=\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\vartheta),\] and in particular \[V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le \mathfrak R_\star .\]

  3. For \(\alpha>0\), define the closed side-law neighborhood value \[V_\alpha(\theta_0) = \inf_{\beta:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]}\ \sup_{\theta:\,D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le \alpha} R_\theta(\beta).\] The required shrinking-neighborhood fact is the reciprocal-radius convergence \[V_{1/(N+1)}(\theta_0)\longrightarrow V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0) \qquad\text{as }N\to\infty .\] Indeed, for every \(\alpha\ge0\), the exact fiber \(\mathcal F(\theta_0)\) is contained in the closed \(\alpha\)-neighborhood, and therefore \[V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le V_\alpha(\theta_0).\] This gives the lower side of the limit. For the upper side, fix a real number \(\mathfrak b>V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\) and put \[\mathfrak c=\frac{V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)+\mathfrak b}{2}, \qquad V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)<\mathfrak c<\mathfrak b.\] Since the fiber risks are nonnegative, the strict infimum property gives a decision \(\beta^\sharp:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\) with \[\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)}R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c,\] so \[\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c.\] For this fixed decision the map \(\theta\mapsto R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)\) is continuous, because \(\mathsf Z\) is finite and the coordinates \(\Lambda_\theta(z)\) and \(\upsilon_\theta\) are continuous. Hence the set \[\mathcal B= \{\theta\in\Theta:\mathfrak c\le R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)\}\] is closed, and compactness of \(\Theta\) makes \(\mathcal B\) compact. The side-law map \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta\) is continuous, so its image \(\Xi(\mathcal B)=\{\Xi_\theta:\theta\in\mathcal B\}\) is compact and therefore closed in the finite simplex. The preceding fiber inequality implies \[\Xi_{\theta_0}\notin \Xi(\mathcal B),\] for otherwise some \(\theta\in\mathcal B\) would satisfy \(\Xi_\theta=\Xi_{\theta_0}\) and hence both \(\mathfrak c\le R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)\) and \(R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c\). Since a closed set misses \(\Xi_{\theta_0}\), there is \(\alpha_0>0\) such that \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})<\alpha_0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c .\] For all sufficiently large \(N\), \(1/(N+1)<\alpha_0\). For such \(N\), every \(\theta\) with \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le \frac1{N+1}\] satisfies \(R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c\), and therefore \[V_{1/(N+1)}(\theta_0) \le \sup_{\theta:\,D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le 1/(N+1)} R_\theta(\beta^\sharp) \le \mathfrak c <\mathfrak b.\] Together with \(V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le V_{1/(N+1)}(\theta_0)\), this proves the displayed convergence.

    Apply the convergence with \(\mathfrak b=V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)+\delta/2\). Choose an integer \(N_{\theta_0}\ge0\) with \[V_{1/(N_{\theta_0}+1)}(\theta_0) < V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)+\delta/2 .\] Set \[r(\theta_0)=\frac1{N_{\theta_0}+1}>0 .\] Together with \(V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le\mathfrak R_\star\), this gives \[V_{r(\theta_0)}(\theta_0)<\mathfrak R_\star+\delta .\] Since all risks are nonnegative, the range of worst-case risks over admissible decisions is bounded below by \(0\). Hence the strict inequality for the infimum yields a decision \[\beta_{\theta_0}:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\] such that \[\sup_{\theta:\,D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le r(\theta_0)} R_\theta(\beta_{\theta_0}) < \mathfrak R_\star+\delta .\] Consequently, whenever \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})<r(\theta_0),\] the same \(\theta\) belongs to the closed neighborhood, and therefore \[R_\theta(\beta_{\theta_0})\le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta .\]

  4. The map \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta\) is continuous because each coordinate \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta(c)\) is continuous and \(\mathsf C\) is finite. For each \(\theta_0\), define \[U_{\theta_0} = \left\{\theta\in\Theta: D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})<\frac{r(\theta_0)}2 \right\}.\] Each \(U_{\theta_0}\) is open, and \(\theta_0\in U_{\theta_0}\). Thus \(\{U_{\theta_0}:\theta_0\in\Theta\}\) is an open cover of \(\Theta\). Compactness gives finitely many centers \[\theta^\circ_1,\ldots,\theta^\circ_k\] such that these sets still cover \(\Theta\). Since \(\Theta\) is nonempty, this finite subcover has \(k\ge1\).

  5. For each selected center set \[\operatorname{rad}_j=r(\theta^\circ_j), \qquad \beta_j=\beta_{\theta^\circ_j}.\] Define \[\eta=\min_{1\le j\le k}\frac{\operatorname{rad}_j}{2}.\] Every \(\operatorname{rad}_j\) is positive, so \(\eta>0\), and \[\eta\le \frac{\operatorname{rad}_j}{2}<\operatorname{rad}_j \qquad\text{for every }j.\] If \(\theta\in\Theta\), the finite subcover gives an index \(j\) with \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j}) < \frac{\operatorname{rad}_j}{2}.\] Since \(\eta\le\operatorname{rad}_j/2\), this implies \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j}) < \operatorname{rad}_j-\eta .\] Finally, if for some \(j\) and \(\theta\), \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j})<\operatorname{rad}_j,\] then the local guarantee from the construction at \(\theta^\circ_j\) gives \[\sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\beta_j(z)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 = R_\theta(\beta_j) \le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta .\] These are exactly the required centers, decisions, radii, and margin.

∎

Lemma 26 supplies the finite selection step used in the side-information limit. Compactness and continuity allow the exact-side decision problem to be represented, up to any tolerance \(\delta\), by finitely many neighborhoods of side laws. The positive margin \(\eta\) gives room for an empirical side table to select one of these neighborhoods uniformly once the auxiliary sample is large.

Lemma 27 [lem:finite-side-information-convergence] (Finite side-information convergence).

Let the overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) satisfy \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), let \(n\ge0\), and let \(d\ge2\). Let \[\Theta_{\epsilon,d} = \Bigl\{(\pi,\varrho,\omega): \pi\in\mathsf{Prob}([d]),\ \varrho_x\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon],\ \omega_{\alpha x}\in[0,1]\ \text{for }x\in[d],\ \alpha\in\{0,1\} \Bigr\}.\] For \(\theta=(\pi,\varrho,\omega)\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), set \[P_\theta(x,\alpha,y) = \pi_x\varrho_x^\alpha(1-\varrho_x)^{1-\alpha} \omega_{\alpha x}^y(1-\omega_{\alpha x})^{1-y},\] let \(\lambda_\theta=P_\theta^{\otimes n}\) be the labeled-sample law on \(([d]\times\{0,1\}^2)^n\), define \[\xi_\theta(x,\alpha)=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}P_\theta(x,\alpha,y), \qquad \upsilon_\theta=\sum_{x\in[d]}\pi_x(\omega_{1x}-\omega_{0x}).\] For each \(m\ge0\), define \[\mathfrak R_m = \inf_{\varphi:([d]\times\{0,1\}^2)^n\times([d]\times\{0,1\})^m\to[-1,1]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \int \bigl(\varphi(Z,U)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\, d\bigl(\lambda_\theta\otimes\xi_\theta^{\otimes m}\bigr)(Z,U),\] and define \[\mathfrak R_\star = \inf_{\psi:([d]\times\{0,1\}^2)^n\times\mathsf{Prob}([d]\times\{0,1\})\to[-1,1]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \int \bigl(\psi(Z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\, d\lambda_\theta(Z).\] Then \[\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m=\mathfrak R_\star .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 27.

Let \[\mathsf Z_n=([d]\times\{0,1\}^2)^n, \qquad \mathsf C_d=[d]\times\{0,1\}.\] For \(\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), write \[\Lambda_\theta(z)=\lambda_\theta(\{z\}),\qquad \Xi_\theta(c)=\xi_\theta(c),\qquad c\in\mathsf C_d .\] The assumptions \(d\ge2\) and \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) make \([d]\) nonempty and make \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\) nonempty. The coordinate map \[(\pi,\varrho,\omega) \longmapsto \Bigl(\pi,\ (\varrho_x)_{x\in[d]},\ (\omega_{\alpha x})_{(\alpha,x)\in\{0,1\}\times[d]}\Bigr)\] identifies \(\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), with its coordinate topology, with the finite product \[\mathsf{Prob}([d]) \times [\epsilon,1-\epsilon]^{[d]} \times [0,1]^{\{0,1\}\times[d]} .\] Here \(\mathsf{Prob}([d])\) is the closed simplex \[\left\{\pi\in[0,1]^{[d]}:\sum_{x\in[d]}\pi_x=1\right\},\] which is compact as a closed subset of the finite cube \([0,1]^{[d]}\). The two interval powers are finite products of compact intervals. Hence \(\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\) is nonempty and compact.

For each \(\theta\), \(\Lambda_\theta\) is a probability law on \(\mathsf Z_n\), because it is the \(n\)-fold product of the one-record law \(P_\theta\). Also \(\Xi_\theta\) is a probability law on \(\mathsf C_d\). Indeed, every summand is nonnegative, and the normalization expands as \[\begin{aligned} \sum_{x\in[d]}\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\Xi_\theta(x,\alpha) &= \sum_{x\in[d]}\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}} \pi_x\varrho_x^\alpha(1-\varrho_x)^{1-\alpha} \omega_{\alpha x}^y(1-\omega_{\alpha x})^{1-y}\\ &= \sum_{x\in[d]}\pi_x \left(\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\varrho_x^\alpha(1-\varrho_x)^{1-\alpha} \left[\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}\omega_{\alpha x}^y(1-\omega_{\alpha x})^{1-y}\right]\right)\\ &= \sum_{x\in[d]}\pi_x \left((1-\varrho_x)+\varrho_x\right) =1 . \end{aligned}\] The maps \(\theta\mapsto\Lambda_\theta(z)\), \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta(c)\), and \(\theta\mapsto\upsilon_\theta\) are continuous, since each is obtained from finitely many coordinate projections by finite sums and products. Finally, \[-1\le \omega_{1x}-\omega_{0x}\le1 \quad\text{for every }x,\] and the weights \(\pi_x\) are nonnegative with total mass one, so \[-1 = \sum_x\pi_x(-1) \le \sum_x\pi_x(\omega_{1x}-\omega_{0x}) = \upsilon_\theta \le \sum_x\pi_x(1) = 1 .\]

With this notation, the empirical-side minimax value at auxiliary size \(m\) is \[\mathfrak R_m = \inf_{\varphi:\mathsf Z_n\times\mathsf C_d^m\to[-1,1]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \sum_{z\in\mathsf Z_n}\Lambda_\theta(z) \sum_{u\in\mathsf C_d^m}\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}(u) \bigl(\varphi(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2,\] and the exact-side minimax value is \[\mathfrak R_\star = \inf_{\psi:\mathsf Z_n\times\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C_d)\to[-1,1]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \sum_{z\in\mathsf Z_n}\Lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\Xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] We now use the finite side-information convergence argument for these data. First, for any empirical estimator \(\varphi:\mathsf Z_n\times\mathsf C_d^m\to[-1,1]\), define the corresponding exact-side estimator \[\overline\varphi(z,\nu) = \sum_{u\in\mathsf C_d^m}\nu^{\otimes m}(u)\,\varphi(z,u), \qquad z\in\mathsf Z_n,\quad \nu\in\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C_d).\] Convexity of \(v\mapsto(v-\upsilon_\theta)^2\) gives, for every \(\theta\), \[\int \bigl(\overline\varphi(Z,\Xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\,d\lambda_\theta(Z) \le \int \bigl(\varphi(Z,U)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\, d\bigl(\lambda_\theta\otimes\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}\bigr)(Z,U).\] Taking suprema over \(\theta\) and then infima over \(\varphi\) yields \[\mathfrak R_\star\le \mathfrak R_m \qquad\text{for every }m\ge0 .\]

It remains to prove the reverse asymptotic inequality. Put \[D_\infty(\nu,\nu')=\max_{c\in\mathsf C_d}|\nu(c)-\nu'(c)|\] for probability laws on \(\mathsf C_d\). Fix \(\delta>0\). With \(\Theta=\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), \(\mathsf Z=\mathsf Z_n\), \(\mathsf C=\mathsf C_d\), \(\Lambda_\theta=\lambda_\theta\), \(\Xi_\theta=\xi_\theta\), \(l=-1\), and \(u=1\), the compactness, simplex, continuity, and target-range checks above give the analytic hypotheses of Lemma 26. Its remaining structural hypotheses are immediate but worth recording: \(\mathsf Z_n\) is a finite power of a finite set, \(\mathsf C_d\) is finite and nonempty because \(d\ge2\), and \(-1\le1\). Thus every hypothesis of Lemma 26 holds. Hence, for the present \(\delta\), there are an integer \(k\ge1\), centers \(\theta^\circ_1,\ldots,\theta^\circ_k\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), bounded decisions \(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_k:\mathsf Z_n\to[-1,1]\), radii \(\operatorname{rad}_1,\ldots,\operatorname{rad}_k\), and a margin \(\eta>0\) such that \[\eta<\operatorname{rad}_j\qquad(1\le j\le k),\] \[\forall\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\quad \exists j\in\{1,\ldots,k\},\qquad D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j}) < \operatorname{rad}_j-\eta,\] and, for every \(j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\) and every \(\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j})<\operatorname{rad}_j \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \int \bigl(\beta_j(Z)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\,d\lambda_\theta(Z) \le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta .\]

For \(m\ge1\) and \(u\in\mathsf C_d^m\), let \(\widehat\Xi_u\in\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C_d)\) be the empirical law of \(u\), and put \(\mathcal J:=\{1,\ldots,k\}\). Since \(k\ge1\), this set is nonempty. Choose a selector \(\sigma:\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C_d)\to\mathcal J\) as follows: if at least one \(\jmath\in\mathcal J\) satisfies \[D_\infty(\nu,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_{\jmath}}) < \operatorname{rad}_{\jmath}-\eta/2,\] let \(\sigma(\nu)\) be the least such index; if there is no such index, set \(\sigma(\nu)=1\). Define \[\Phi_{\delta,m}(z,u)=\beta_{\sigma(\widehat\Xi_u)}(z).\] On the event \[G_{\theta,m} = \left\{ u\in\mathsf C_d^m: D_\infty(\widehat\Xi_u,\Xi_\theta)<\eta/2 \right\},\] choose a cover index \(j\in\mathcal J\) with \[D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_j^\circ}) <\operatorname{rad}_j-\eta.\] The good-event bound and the triangle inequality give \[D_\infty(\widehat\Xi_u,\Xi_{\theta_j^\circ}) <\eta/2+\operatorname{rad}_j-\eta =\operatorname{rad}_j-\eta/2,\] so \(j\) is eligible for the selector. Hence \[D_\infty\bigl(\widehat\Xi_u, \Xi_{\theta^\circ_{\sigma(\widehat\Xi_u)}}\bigr) <\operatorname{rad}_{\sigma(\widehat\Xi_u)}-\eta/2.\] Applying the triangle inequality once more, now through \(\widehat\Xi_u\), yields \[D_\infty\bigl(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_{\sigma(\widehat\Xi_u)}}\bigr) < \eta/2+\operatorname{rad}_{\sigma(\widehat\Xi_u)}-\eta/2 = \operatorname{rad}_{\sigma(\widehat\Xi_u)}.\] Therefore \[\int \bigl(\Phi_{\delta,m}(Z,U)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\, d(\lambda_\theta\otimes\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m})(Z,U) \le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta + 4\,\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}(G_{\theta,m}^{c}),\] because all estimates and all targets lie in \([-1,1]\). For the finite-alphabet concentration step, fix \(\theta\). For \(c\in\mathsf C_d\) and \(u=(u_1,\ldots,u_m)\in\mathsf C_d^m\), write \[N_c(u)=\sum_{i=1}^m\mathbf 1\{u_i=c\}, \qquad \widehat\Xi_u(c)=\frac{N_c(u)}m .\] Under \(\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}\), the variables \(\mathbf 1\{U_i=c\}\) are independent Bernoulli variables with mean \(\Xi_\theta(c)\). Thus \[\mathbb E_\theta\widehat\Xi_U(c)=\Xi_\theta(c)\] and, since a Bernoulli variance is at most \(1/4\), \[\operatorname{Var}_\theta\!\left(\widehat\Xi_U(c)\right) = \frac{1}{m^2}\sum_{i=1}^m \operatorname{Var}_\theta\!\left(\mathbf 1\{U_i=c\}\right) \le \frac{1}{4m} .\] Chebyshev’s inequality gives, for every \(t>0\), \[\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}\left( \left|\widehat\Xi_U(c)-\Xi_\theta(c)\right| \ge t \right) \le \frac{1}{4mt^2} .\] If \(D_\infty(\widehat\Xi_U,\Xi_\theta)\ge\eta/2\), then the defining identity \[D_\infty(\widehat\Xi_U,\Xi_\theta) = \max_{c\in\mathsf C_d} \left|\widehat\Xi_U(c)-\Xi_\theta(c)\right|\] implies that some \(c\in\mathsf C_d\) satisfies \[\left|\widehat\Xi_U(c)-\Xi_\theta(c)\right| \ge \eta/2 .\] Hence the bad event satisfies \[G_{\theta,m}^c = \left\{D_\infty(\widehat\Xi_U,\Xi_\theta)\ge\eta/2\right\} \subseteq \bigcup_{c\in\mathsf C_d} \left\{ \left|\widehat\Xi_U(c)-\Xi_\theta(c)\right| \ge \eta/2 \right\}.\] The union bound over the finite alphabet gives \[\Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}(G_{\theta,m}^{c}) \le \sum_{c\in\mathsf C_d} \Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}\left( \left|\widehat\Xi_U(c)-\Xi_\theta(c)\right| \ge \eta/2 \right) \le \frac{|\mathsf C_d|}{m\eta^2} .\] The right-hand side is independent of \(\theta\) and tends to zero, hence \[\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \Xi_\theta^{\otimes m}(G_{\theta,m}^{c}) \le \frac{|\mathsf C_d|}{m\eta^2} \longrightarrow0 .\]

Taking the supremum over \(\theta\) and then using the displayed estimator \(\Phi_{\delta,m}\) in the minimax infimum yields \[\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m\le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta .\] Since \(\delta>0\) was arbitrary, \[\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m\le \mathfrak R_\star .\]

Combining \(\mathfrak R_\star\le\mathfrak R_m\) for every \(m\) with the last display gives \[\liminf_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m\ge\mathfrak R_\star \qquad\text{and}\qquad \limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m\le\mathfrak R_\star .\] Thus \(\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m=\mathfrak R_\star\).

∎

Lemma 27 states the population-side-information approximation directly: as the number of auxiliary treatment–covariate records grows, the finite-sample minimax risk converges to the risk obtained when the treatment–covariate marginal is observed exactly. This supplies the finite-dimensional continuity step behind the known-marginal limit in Theorem 5 and keeps the benchmark comparison on the same labeled-sample scale as the annotation experiment.

Finite-cover selection input.

The finite-cover mechanism in Lemma 26 applies to the discrete causal parametrization because the parameter set is compact and its labeled probabilities, side-law probabilities, and target are continuous functions of the cell probabilities. Concentration of the empirical side table then selects a valid neighborhood with probability tending uniformly to one, while Lemma 28 gives the complementary comparison from an empirical side sample to its exact probability vector.

Lemma 28 [lem:finite-side-measurable-comparison] (Empirical side comparison).

Let \(\Theta\) be a nonempty parameter set, let \(\mathcal Z\) and \(\mathcal C\) be nonempty finite sets, and let \(\beta_-\le \beta_+\) be real numbers. Suppose that, for each \(\theta\in\Theta\), the following objects are given:

  • (Observed side law.) A probability vector \(\lambda_\theta\) on \(\mathcal Z\).

  • (Empirical side law.) A probability vector \(\xi_\theta\) on \(\mathcal C\).

  • (Target.) A number \(\upsilon_\theta\in[\beta_-,\beta_+]\).

For each integer \(m\ge0\), define \[\mathfrak R_m = \inf_{\varphi:\mathcal Z\times\mathcal C^m\to[\beta_-,\beta_+]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z}\lambda_\theta(z) \sum_{v\in\mathcal C^m} \left(\prod_{j=1}^m \xi_\theta(v_j)\right) \bigl(\varphi(z,v)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] Define also \[\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star = \inf_{\psi} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z}\lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 ,\] where the infimum ranges over all maps \[\psi:\mathcal Z\times\mathbb R^{\mathcal C}\to[\beta_-,\beta_+]\] such that, for each \(z\in\mathcal Z\), the section \(w\mapsto\psi(z,w)\) is Borel measurable. Then, for every integer \(m\ge0\), \[\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\le \mathfrak R_m .\]

⊢ Lean

Lemma 28 gives the corresponding monotone comparison for finite spaces: replacing an empirical side sample by its exact probability vector weakly lowers the minimax risk. In the known-marginal application, this comparison expresses the benchmark as the risk associated with observing the treatment–covariate law itself, rather than a random finite sample from that law.

A complementary asymptotic comparison spells out when auxiliary annotation improves the rate in Definition 12 relative to the labeled-only case. It expresses the improvement entirely through the alphabet growth and the effective treatment–covariate sample size.

Lemma 29 [lem:annotation-frontier-strict-improvement] (Strict frontier improvement).

Let \((m_n)_{n\ge 1}\) and \((d_n)_{n\ge 1}\) be sequences of nonnegative integers such that \(d_n\ge 2\) for every \(n\ge 1\). For each \(n\ge 1\), set \(N_n=n+m_n\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\), and let \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) be the annotation-frontier rate in Definition 12. Then, as \(n\to\infty\), \[r_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n) = o\!\left(r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)\right)\] if and only if \[\frac{d_n}{\sqrt n\,\ell_n}\to\infty\] and \[\frac{d_n/(N_n\ell_n)} {\min\{1,d_n/(n\ell_n)\}} \to 0.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 29.

  1. All limits are taken as \(n\to\infty\), and changing finitely many initial values is immaterial. Put \[\iota_n:=\frac1n,\qquad \alpha_n:=\frac{d_n}{n\ell_n},\qquad \beta_n:=\frac{d_n}{N_n\ell_n}.\] For every sufficiently large \(n\), \(\ell_n=\log(en)>0\), \(0<\iota_n\le 1\), \(\alpha_n>0\), \(\beta_n\ge0\), and \(\beta_n\le\alpha_n\). The last inequality uses \(N_n=n+m_n\ge n\), while positivity of \(\alpha_n\) uses \(d_n\ge2\). By Definition 12, \[r_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)=\min\{1,\iota_n+\beta_n^2\}, \qquad r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)=\min\{1,\iota_n+\alpha_n^2\}.\]

  2. We use the following numerical reduction. Suppose sequences \(\iota_n,\alpha_n,\beta_n\) satisfy, eventually, \[0<\iota_n\le1,\qquad 0<\alpha_n,\qquad 0\le\beta_n\le\alpha_n.\] Define \[\mathcal R_n:=\min\{1,\iota_n+\beta_n^2\},\qquad \mathcal R^0_n:=\min\{1,\iota_n+\alpha_n^2\},\qquad \mathcal H_n:=\min\{1,\alpha_n\}.\] Then \[\mathcal R_n=o(\mathcal R^0_n) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal R^0_n}\to0 \ \text{ and }\ \frac{\beta_n}{\mathcal H_n}\to0.\] Indeed, if \(\mathcal R_n=o(\mathcal R^0_n)\), then \(\mathcal R_n/\mathcal R^0_n\to0\). Since \(\iota_n\le\mathcal R_n\) and \(\mathcal R^0_n>0\) eventually, \[0\le\frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal R^0_n} \le \frac{\mathcal R_n}{\mathcal R^0_n},\] so \(\iota_n/\mathcal R^0_n\to0\). Also \(\mathcal R_n/\mathcal R^0_n<1\) eventually; hence \(\mathcal R_n<1\) eventually, and therefore \[\mathcal R_n=\iota_n+\beta_n^2\] eventually. Thus \[0\le \frac{\beta_n^2}{\mathcal R^0_n} \le \frac{\mathcal R_n}{\mathcal R^0_n},\] so \(\beta_n^2/\mathcal R^0_n\to0\). To replace \(\mathcal R^0_n\) by \(\mathcal H_n^2\), observe that \[\mathcal H_n^2=\min\{1,\alpha_n^2\},\] and hence \[\mathcal H_n^2\le \mathcal R^0_n\le \iota_n+\mathcal H_n^2.\] Writing \(\mathcal Q_n:=\iota_n/\mathcal R^0_n\), we have \(\mathcal Q_n\to0\), and whenever \(\mathcal Q_n<1\), \[0\le \frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal H_n^2} \le \frac{\mathcal Q_n}{1-\mathcal Q_n}.\] Consequently \(\iota_n/\mathcal H_n^2\to0\), and the displayed two-sided bound gives \[\frac{\mathcal R^0_n}{\mathcal H_n^2}\to1.\] Therefore \[\frac{\beta_n^2}{\mathcal H_n^2} = \frac{\beta_n^2}{\mathcal R^0_n}\, \frac{\mathcal R^0_n}{\mathcal H_n^2} \to0,\] and taking square roots, using \(\beta_n\ge0\) and \(\mathcal H_n>0\), yields \(\beta_n/\mathcal H_n\to0\).

    Conversely, assume \[\frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal R^0_n}\to0, \qquad \frac{\beta_n}{\mathcal H_n}\to0.\] Then \(\beta_n^2/\mathcal H_n^2\to0\), and since \(\mathcal H_n^2\le\mathcal R^0_n\), \[0\le \frac{\beta_n^2}{\mathcal R^0_n} \le \frac{\beta_n^2}{\mathcal H_n^2} \to0.\] Finally, \[0\le \frac{\mathcal R_n}{\mathcal R^0_n} \le \frac{\iota_n+\beta_n^2}{\mathcal R^0_n} = \frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal R^0_n} + \frac{\beta_n^2}{\mathcal R^0_n} \to0,\] which is exactly \(\mathcal R_n=o(\mathcal R^0_n)\).

  3. We also need the supervised floor equivalence \[\frac{(1/n)}{r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)}\to0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \frac{d_n}{\sqrt n\,\ell_n}\to\infty.\] Set \[\sigma_n:=\frac{d_n}{\sqrt n\,\ell_n}.\] For \(n\ge1\), \[r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)=\min\{1,\iota_n+\alpha_n^2\},\] and direct algebra gives \[\frac{\iota_n}{\iota_n+\alpha_n^2} = \frac1{1+\sigma_n^2}.\] Assume first that \(\iota_n/r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)\to0\). Given a real threshold \(Y\), put \[Y_+:=\max\{Y,0\}+1.\] Eventually \[\frac{\iota_n}{r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)} < \frac1{1+Y_+^2}.\] Since \(r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)\le \iota_n+\alpha_n^2\), \[\frac1{1+\sigma_n^2} = \frac{\iota_n}{\iota_n+\alpha_n^2} \le \frac{\iota_n}{r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)} < \frac1{1+Y_+^2}.\] Because \(\sigma_n\ge0\), this forces \(\sigma_n\ge Y_+\), and hence \(\sigma_n\ge Y\) eventually. Thus \(\sigma_n\to\infty\).

    Conversely, if \(\sigma_n\to\infty\), then \(\iota_n\to0\) and \(1/\sigma_n^2\to0\). With \[\mathcal H_n:=\min\{1,\alpha_n\},\] the same identity \(\mathcal H_n^2=\min\{1,\alpha_n^2\}\) gives \[\mathcal H_n^2\le r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n).\] Therefore \[\frac{\iota_n}{r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)} \le \frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal H_n^2}.\] If \(\alpha_n\le1\), then \[\frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal H_n^2} = \frac{\iota_n}{\alpha_n^2} = \frac1{\sigma_n^2};\] if \(\alpha_n>1\), then \(\mathcal H_n=1\) and \[\frac{\iota_n}{\mathcal H_n^2} = \iota_n \le \iota_n+\frac1{\sigma_n^2}.\] Thus, in all cases, \[0\le \frac{\iota_n}{r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)} \le \iota_n+\frac1{\sigma_n^2}\to0,\] which proves the equivalence.

  4. Applying the reduction from the second step to the sequences in the first step yields \[r_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)=o\!\left(r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)\right)\] if and only if \[\frac{\iota_n}{r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)}\to0 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \frac{\beta_n}{\min\{1,\alpha_n\}}\to0.\] Substituting the definitions of \(\alpha_n\) and \(\beta_n\), the second condition is exactly \[\frac{d_n/(N_n\ell_n)} {\min\{1,d_n/(n\ell_n)\}} \to0.\] By the supervised floor equivalence from the third step, the first condition is exactly \[\frac{d_n}{\sqrt n\,\ell_n}\to\infty.\] Combining the two equivalences proves the claimed characterization.

∎

Lemma 29 identifies the regimes in which the annotation rate is asymptotically smaller than the labeled-only rate. The first condition places the alphabet beyond the labeled-only parametric scale, so treatment–covariate information can affect the leading rate. The second condition requires the combined treatment–covariate sample size to reduce the alphabet-dependent term relative to its labeled-only analogue. Together these conditions give an exact rate-level criterion for when auxiliary annotation changes the asymptotic order delivered by Theorem 4.
