Proofs of the main results

Proof of Theorem 1.

Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\). We prove the result with \[c_\epsilon=\frac1{100}, \qquad C_\epsilon=2\epsilon^{-1}.\] The first constant is positive, and \(c_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon\) because \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) gives \[\frac1{100}\le \frac{2}{\epsilon} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \frac{\epsilon}{100}\le 2 .\] It remains to verify the two risk inequalities uniformly over \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\).

  1. For the lower bound, put \[\delta:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n}.\] Then \(\delta\ge0\), \(\delta\le2/5\), and \[\delta^2=\frac{4}{25n}.\] Consider two laws on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\). Under \(P_{\circ}\), the covariate is uniform on \([d]\), the propensity is \(1/2\), and both outcome regressions equal \(1/2\). Under \(P_{\delta}\), the covariate is again uniform, the propensity is \(1/2\), the control regression is \(1/2\), and the treated regression is \(1/2+\delta\). Since \(\delta\le2/5\), the perturbed treated regression lies in \([0,1]\). Both laws belong to \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) from Definition 6, because their occupied-cell propensity is \(1/2\), which satisfies the overlap condition in Assumption 1.

  2. The two laws have the same supplied treatment-covariate table: \[(P_{\circ})_{XA}(x,a)=\frac{1}{2d}=(P_{\delta})_{XA}(x,a), \qquad x\in[d],\ a\in\{0,1\}.\] Their ATE values, computed from Definition 8, are \[\tau(P_{\circ})=0, \qquad \tau(P_{\delta}) =\sum_{x=1}^d \frac1d\Bigl(\frac12+\delta-\frac12\Bigr) =\delta .\] Thus, with the testing half-separation \[\eta_{\mathrm{test}}:=\frac{\delta}{2},\] we have \[2\eta_{\mathrm{test}}=\delta =\bigl|\tau(P_{\circ})-\tau(P_{\delta})\bigr|.\]

  3. The product laws are sufficiently close in total variation. For one labeled observation, with the perturbed law compared to the null law, the chi-squared increment is \[\chi_1^2 := \frac{(1/2)\delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)} =2\delta^2 =\frac{8}{25n}.\] Thus \[n\chi_1^2=\frac{8}{25}\le \log 2 .\] The product chi-squared divergence tensorizes as \[1+\chi^2\!\left(P_{\delta}^{\otimes n}\Vert P_{\circ}^{\otimes n}\right) =\bigl(1+\chi_1^2\bigr)^n .\] Since \(1+u\le e^u\) for \(u\ge0\), the preceding calibration gives \[1+\chi^2\!\left(P_{\delta}^{\otimes n}\Vert P_{\circ}^{\otimes n}\right) \le \exp(n\chi_1^2) \le \exp(\log 2)=2,\] and hence \[\chi^2\!\left(P_{\delta}^{\otimes n}\Vert P_{\circ}^{\otimes n}\right) \le 1.\] The perturbed product law is absolutely continuous with respect to the null product law because every one-observation atom under the null has positive mass. Therefore the standard Cauchy–Schwarz comparison between total variation and chi-squared divergence yields \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(P_{\circ}^{\otimes n},P_{\delta}^{\otimes n}\right) = \operatorname{TV}\!\left(P_{\delta}^{\otimes n},P_{\circ}^{\otimes n}\right) \le \frac12 \sqrt{\chi^2\!\left(P_{\delta}^{\otimes n}\Vert P_{\circ}^{\otimes n}\right)} \le \frac12 .\]

  4. Let \(T\in\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}\) be any known-marginal estimator, and define \[f(\mathcal L_n):= T\!\left(\mathcal L_n,(P_{\circ})_{XA}\right) = T\!\left(\mathcal L_n,(P_{\delta})_{XA}\right),\] where the equality uses the common supplied table. Since the two targets are separated by \(2\eta_{\mathrm{test}}\), for every sample point at least one of \[\bigl|f-\tau(P_{\circ})\bigr|\ge \eta_{\mathrm{test}}, \qquad \bigl|f-\tau(P_{\delta})\bigr|\ge \eta_{\mathrm{test}}\] holds. The total-variation bound therefore gives \[\max\!\left\{ P_{\circ}^{\otimes n}\!\left(\bigl|f-\tau(P_{\circ})\bigr|\ge \eta_{\mathrm{test}}\right), P_{\delta}^{\otimes n}\!\left(\bigl|f-\tau(P_{\delta})\bigr|\ge \eta_{\mathrm{test}}\right) \right\} \ge \frac{1-\operatorname{TV}(P_{\circ}^{\otimes n},P_{\delta}^{\otimes n})}{2} \ge \frac14 .\] For either law \(P_\star\in\{P_{\circ},P_{\delta}\}\), \[\eta_{\mathrm{test}}^2\, P_\star^{\otimes n}\!\left(\bigl|f-\tau(P_\star)\bigr|\ge \eta_{\mathrm{test}}\right) \le \mathbb E_{P_\star^{\otimes n}} \!\left[\bigl(T(\mathcal L_n,(P_\star)_{XA})-\tau(P_\star)\bigr)^2\right].\] Taking the larger of the two risks, then the supremum over \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) and the infimum in Definition 15, yields \[R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d) \ge \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{test}}^2}{4} = \frac{\delta^2}{16} = \frac{1}{100n}.\]

  5. For the upper bound, take any \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). For a supplied table \(\Lambda=(\Lambda_{x,a})_{x,a}\), define the known-marginal score on a record \((x,a,y)\) by \[\psi_{\Lambda}(x,a,y) := \begin{cases} (\Lambda_{x,0}+\Lambda_{x,1})/\Lambda_{x,1}, & y=1,\ a=1,\\ -(\Lambda_{x,0}+\Lambda_{x,1})/\Lambda_{x,0}, & y=1,\ a=0,\\ 0, & y=0, \end{cases}\] with any ratio having denominator \(0\) assigned value \(0\). The raw estimator is \[\overline\psi_{\Lambda}(\mathcal L_n) := \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n \psi_{\Lambda}(X_i,A_i,Y_i),\] and the estimator used in the risk is its projection to \([-1,1]\). This projection is measurable on the finite sample space and weakly decreases squared loss because \(\tau(P)\in[-1,1]\).

  6. Now specialize the table to the true table \(P_{XA}\). Write \[p_x=P(X=x),\qquad s_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a),\qquad q_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a,Y=1).\] If \(p_x>0\), overlap gives \[\epsilon p_x\le s_{1x}, \qquad \epsilon p_x\le s_{0x};\] if \(p_x=0\), then \(s_{0x}=s_{1x}=q_{0x}=q_{1x}=0\). Hence \[\mathbb E_P[\psi_{P_{XA}}(X,A,Y)] = \sum_x\left(q_{1x}\frac{p_x}{s_{1x}}-q_{0x}\frac{p_x}{s_{0x}}\right) = \sum_x p_x(\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x}) = \tau(P),\] with zero-mass cells contributing \(0\). Similarly, \[\begin{aligned} \mathbb E_P[\psi_{P_{XA}}(X,A,Y)^2] &= \sum_x\left\{ q_{1x}\left(\frac{p_x}{s_{1x}}\right)^2 + q_{0x}\left(\frac{p_x}{s_{0x}}\right)^2 \right\} \\ &\le \sum_x\left\{ \frac{p_x^2}{s_{1x}} + \frac{p_x^2}{s_{0x}} \right\} \le \sum_x 2\epsilon^{-1}p_x = 2\epsilon^{-1}. \end{aligned}\] Thus \[\operatorname{Var}_P\!\left(\psi_{P_{XA}}(X,A,Y)\right) \le 2\epsilon^{-1}.\]

  7. By the labeled product sampling in Assumption 2, \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\overline\psi_{P_{XA}}(\mathcal L_n)\right] =\tau(P), \qquad \operatorname{Var}_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left(\overline\psi_{P_{XA}}(\mathcal L_n)\right) \le \frac{2\epsilon^{-1}}{n}.\] After clipping, the squared risk is bounded by the same quantity. Taking the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) and then the infimum over estimators in Definition 15 gives \[R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d) \le \frac{2\epsilon^{-1}}{n} = \frac{C_\epsilon}{n}.\] Together with the lower bound, this proves the claimed inequalities for all \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\).

∎
Proof of Theorem 2.

Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).

  1. By Lemma 14, choose a universal constant \(C_{\mathrm{tr}}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), \(d\ge2\), and \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le 2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon) +\frac{C_{\mathrm{tr}}}{n}.\] By Lemma 13, choose \(C_{\mathrm{Pois},\epsilon}>0\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that, for the same range of parameters and laws, \[\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon) \le C_{\mathrm{Pois},\epsilon}\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Set \[C_\epsilon:=2C_{\mathrm{Pois},\epsilon}+C_{\mathrm{tr}}.\] Then \(C_\epsilon>0\).

  2. It remains to verify the displayed bound uniformly over \(P\). Fix \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\). For any \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), the two estimates above give \[\begin{aligned} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] &\le 2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon) +\frac{C_{\mathrm{tr}}}{n} \\ &\le 2C_{\mathrm{Pois},\epsilon}\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d) +C_{\mathrm{tr}}\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d). \end{aligned}\] The last inequality uses the elementary rate comparison \[\frac1n\le r_\epsilon(n,m,d),\] which follows directly from Definition 12, since \[\frac1n \le \min\!\left\{ 1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\ell_n^2} \right\} = r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\] for \(n\ge1\). Therefore \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le (2C_{\mathrm{Pois},\epsilon}+C_{\mathrm{tr}})\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d) = C_\epsilon\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d).\]

  3. Since the preceding bound holds for every \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), taking the supremum over the model class yields \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le C_\epsilon\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] The rate is positive for \(n\ge1\) by Definition 12, so the right-hand side is a well-defined finite real number. The asserted uniform bound follows for all \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\).

∎
Proof of Theorem 3.

  1. Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\). By Lemma 25, there are positive numerical constants \[C_{\mathrm{fix}}>0,\qquad c_{\mathrm{risk}}>0,\] and, at this \(\epsilon\), calibration constants as in Definition 19, together with \[\rho\in(0,1),\qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}>0, \qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}\le c_{\mathrm{risk}}.\] Write \(C_d>0\) for the dimension constant in this calibration, and write \(c_{\mathrm{gap}}>0\) for the calibration’s dual-gap constant. For every \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), Lemma 25 supplies a handle \(H\) satisfying \[\mathcal L_{\Pi_0(H)}(P_{XA}) = \mathcal L_{\Pi_1(H)}(P_{XA})\] and the floor certificate \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] For this handle, put \[D(n,m,d):= \min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log(en)^2}\right\}\] and \[\Delta_H:=c_{\mathrm{gap}}\,k(H)\,a(H),\] where \(k(H)\) and \(a(H)\) are the rare-cell count and shift in Definition 19. In the finite-atom branch, the same cited certificate gives the nonparametric dimension comparison \[C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le \Delta_H^2\] and the fixed-sample lower bound attached to the canonical handle, \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}\Delta_H^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] The displayed lower bound follows once the handle’s common-marginal witness, total-variation bound, center separation, and variance absorption satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma 21. In the complementary branch, Lemma 25 gives directly \[C_dD(n,m,d)\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}.\]

  2. Define \[c_{\mathrm{lb}}:= \min\left\{ \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{2}, \frac{C_d}{2}, \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}C_d}{2} \right\}.\] Since \(c_{\mathrm{floor}}>0\) and \(C_d>0\), this constant is positive.

  3. Fix \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), and take the handle \(H\) from the certificate package. Put \[Q(n,m,d):= \frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log(en)^2}, \qquad D(n,m,d)=\min\{1,Q(n,m,d)\}.\] Both \(Q(n,m,d)\) and \(D(n,m,d)\) are nonnegative. The frontier rate in Definition 12 satisfies \[r_\epsilon(n,m,d) = \min\left\{1,\frac1n+Q(n,m,d)\right\} \le \frac1n+D(n,m,d).\] Indeed, if \(Q(n,m,d)\le1\), the right side is \(1/n+Q(n,m,d)\), which dominates the minimum. If \(Q(n,m,d)>1\), then \(D(n,m,d)=1\), and the left side is at most \(1\le 1/n+1\).

  4. Suppose first that \(H\) is in the finite-atom branch. Since \(c_{\mathrm{floor}}>0\), multiplying the finite-branch dimension comparison by \(c_{\mathrm{floor}}\) and then using the transferred fixed-sample lower bound gives \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}C_dD(n,m,d) \le c_{\mathrm{floor}}\Delta_H^2 \le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Together with the floor inequality, \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),\] this supplies the two lower bounds used in the averaging step. Using the definition of \(c_{\mathrm{lb}}\) and the bound from the previous step, \[\begin{aligned} c_{\mathrm{lb}} r_\epsilon(n,m,d) &\le c_{\mathrm{lb}}\left(\frac1n+D(n,m,d)\right)\\ &= c_{\mathrm{lb}}\frac1n+c_{\mathrm{lb}} D(n,m,d)\\ &\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{2}\frac1n +\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}C_d}{2}D(n,m,d)\\ &= \frac12\left( c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n + c_{\mathrm{floor}}C_dD(n,m,d) \right)\\ &\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d). \end{aligned}\] Thus the asserted lower bound holds in the finite-atom branch.

  5. It remains to treat the complementary branch. In this branch, the certificate clause in Lemma 25 gives directly \[C_dD(n,m,d) \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n} = c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n.\] Together with the floor inequality \[c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),\] we obtain \[\begin{aligned} c_{\mathrm{lb}} r_\epsilon(n,m,d) &\le c_{\mathrm{lb}}\left(\frac1n+D(n,m,d)\right)\\ &= c_{\mathrm{lb}}\frac1n+c_{\mathrm{lb}} D(n,m,d)\\ &\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{2}\frac1n +\frac{C_d}{2}D(n,m,d)\\ &\le \frac12\left( c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n+ c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n \right)\\ &= c_{\mathrm{floor}}\frac1n\\ &\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d). \end{aligned}\] This proves the risk lower bound in the complementary branch as well.

  6. Combining the two branches, for every \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), the handle \(H\) supplies priors \(\Pi_0(H)\) and \(\Pi_1(H)\) with common random treatment-covariate marginal law and satisfies \[c_{\mathrm{lb}} r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Taking the theorem’s lower-bound constant \(c_\epsilon\) to be \(c_{\mathrm{lb}}\), and using \(c_{\mathrm{lb}}>0\) with the fixed calibration constants chosen above, gives the asserted conclusion.

∎
Proof of Theorem 4.

  1. By Theorem 3, choose a constant \(c_\epsilon>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), \[c_\epsilon r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] By Theorem 2, choose \(C_\epsilon^{\mathrm{up}}>0\) such that \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}} E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le C_\epsilon^{\mathrm{up}} r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\] for the same range of \(n,m,d\). Set \[C_\epsilon:=\max\{c_\epsilon,C_\epsilon^{\mathrm{up}}\}.\] Then \(0<c_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon\). The estimator \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}\) is measurable, so the definition of \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) in Definition 11 gives \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \le \sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}} E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right].\] Combining the last two displays, and using \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)>0\), yields \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \le C_\epsilon^{\mathrm{up}} r_\epsilon(n,m,d) \le C_\epsilon r_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] Together with the lower bound from Theorem 3, this proves the finite-sample bracket.

  2. The unannotated endpoint is the specialization \(m=0\) of Definition 12. Since \(N=n\) in that case, \[r_\epsilon(n,0,d) = \min\!\left\{1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right\}.\]

  3. The known-marginal limit is exactly the conclusion of Theorem 5: for fixed \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), \[\lim_{m\to\infty} R_\epsilon(n,m,d) = R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d).\]

  4. It remains to record the sequence consequences. Fix sequences \((m_n)\) and \((d_n)\) with \(d_n\ge2\), and set \[\rho_n:=R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n),\qquad q_n:=r_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n),\] \[\rho_n^{0}:=R_\epsilon(n,0,d_n),\qquad q_n^{0}:=r_\epsilon(n,0,d_n).\] The finite-sample bracket gives, for all \(n\ge1\), \[c_\epsilon q_n\le \rho_n\le C_\epsilon q_n, \qquad c_\epsilon q_n^{0}\le \rho_n^{0}\le C_\epsilon q_n^{0}.\] Since all four sequences are nonnegative, these two-sided bounds imply \[(\rho_n)=\Theta(q_n), \qquad (\rho_n^{0})=\Theta(q_n^{0}).\] Indeed, the upper inequalities give the \(O\)-bounds in one direction, and the lower inequalities give \(q_n\le c_\epsilon^{-1}\rho_n\) and \(q_n^{0}\le c_\epsilon^{-1}\rho_n^{0}\).

    With \[N_n=n+m_n,\qquad b_n:=\frac{d_n}{N_n\ell_n},\] Definition 12 gives \[q_n=\min\left\{1,\frac1n+b_n^2\right\}.\] Because \(n^{-1}\to0\), this display satisfies \[q_n\to0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad b_n\to0.\] The implication from right to left follows by continuity of \(x\mapsto\min\{1,x\}\); the implication from left to right follows because eventually \(q_n<1/2\), hence \[q_n=\frac1n+b_n^2\] eventually, so \(b_n^2\to0\). Using \((\rho_n)=\Theta(q_n)\), we obtain \[R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)\to0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \frac{d_n}{N_n\ell_n}\to0.\]

    Next, \[q_n=O(n^{-1}) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad b_n^2=O(n^{-1}) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d_n=O\!\left(\frac{N_n\ell_n}{\sqrt n}\right).\] For the first equivalence, the term \(n^{-1}\) is already present in \(q_n\), and the same eventual equality \(q_n=n^{-1}+b_n^2\) applies after the \(O(n^{-1})\) bound forces \(q_n\to0\). The second equivalence is obtained by taking square roots of nonnegative sequences and multiplying by \(N_n\ell_n\). Transferring through \((\rho_n)=\Theta(q_n)\) gives \[R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)=O(n^{-1}) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d_n=O\!\left(\frac{N_n\ell_n}{\sqrt n}\right).\]

    Finally put \[a_n:=\frac{d_n}{n\ell_n}.\] Then \[q_n=\min\left\{1,\frac1n+b_n^2\right\}, \qquad q_n^{0}=\min\left\{1,\frac1n+a_n^2\right\}, \qquad 0\le b_n\le a_n .\] By Lemma 29, the numerical strict-improvement criterion for these two rates is \[q_n=o(q_n^{0})\] if and only if both \[\frac{d_n}{\sqrt n\,\ell_n}\to\infty\] and \[\frac{b_n}{\min\{1,a_n\}}\to0.\] To see this, observe first that \(q_n\ge n^{-1}\), so \(q_n=o(q_n^{0})\) forces \(n^{-1}=o(q_n^{0})\), which is equivalent to \(a_n\sqrt n=d_n/(\sqrt n\,\ell_n)\to\infty\). Under this condition the floor term \(n^{-1}\) is negligible in \(q_n^{0}\), and \[q_n^{0}\asymp \min\{1,a_n^2\}.\] Since \(0\le b_n\le a_n\), the remaining requirement \(\min\{1,n^{-1}+b_n^2\}=o(q_n^{0})\) is equivalent to \[b_n^2=o(\min\{1,a_n^2\}),\] which, for nonnegative sequences, is the same as \[\frac{b_n}{\min\{1,a_n\}}\to0.\] Substituting the definitions of \(a_n\) and \(b_n\), this last condition is exactly \[\frac{d_n/(N_n\ell_n)} {\min\{1,d_n/(n\ell_n)\}} \to0.\] The two-sided bounds \((\rho_n)=\Theta(q_n)\) and \((\rho_n^{0})=\Theta(q_n^{0})\) transfer \(q_n=o(q_n^{0})\) precisely to \[R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n) = o\!\left(R_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)\right),\] which proves the stated strict-improvement equivalence.

∎
Proof of Theorem 5.

Let \[\mathcal Z_n:=([d]\times\{0,1\}^2)^n, \qquad \mathcal C_d:=[d]\times\{0,1\}.\] We use the compact coordinate space \[\Theta_{\epsilon,d} = \Bigl\{(\pi,\varrho,\omega): \pi\in\mathsf{Prob}([d]),\ \varrho_x\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon],\ \omega_{\alpha x}\in[0,1]\Bigr\}.\] The assumptions \(d\ge2\) and \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) make this space nonempty. For \(\theta=(\pi,\varrho,\omega)\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), set \[P_\theta(x,\alpha,y) = \pi_x\varrho_x^\alpha(1-\varrho_x)^{1-\alpha} \omega_{\alpha x}^y(1-\omega_{\alpha x})^{1-y},\] and define \[\lambda_\theta(z) = \prod_{i=1}^n P_\theta(z_i), \qquad \xi_\theta(x,\alpha) = \sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}P_\theta(x,\alpha,y), \qquad \upsilon_\theta = \sum_{x\in[d]}\pi_x(\omega_{1x}-\omega_{0x}).\] Also, \[-1 = \sum_x\pi_x(-1) \le \sum_x\pi_x(\omega_{1x}-\omega_{0x}) \le \sum_x\pi_x(1) = 1,\] so \(\upsilon_\theta\in[-1,1]\) for every \(\theta\).

  1. Define the finite-side values \[\mathfrak R_m = \inf_{\varphi:\mathcal Z_n\times\mathcal C_d^m\to[-1,1]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m\xi_\theta(u_j)\Bigr) \bigl(\varphi(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\] and \[\mathfrak R_\star = \inf_{\psi:\mathcal Z_n\times\mathsf{Prob}(\mathcal C_d)\to[-1,1]} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] With the preceding coordinates, Lemma 27 gives \[\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m=\mathfrak R_\star .\]

  2. Let \(\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\) be the measurable ambient exact-table value \[\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star = \inf_{\psi} \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2,\] where the infimum ranges over all rules \(\psi:\mathcal Z_n\times\mathbb R^{\mathcal C_d}\to[-1,1]\) whose section \(w\mapsto\psi(z,w)\) is Borel measurable for each \(z\). The target range established above gives \(\upsilon_\theta\in[-1,1]\), and Lemma 27 supplies the convergence \[\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m=\mathfrak R_\star .\] The measurable ambient exact-table value is tied to this limiting sequence by two comparisons. The exact-side value infimizes over all rules on \(\mathsf{Prob}(\mathcal C_d)\), while the ambient value infimizes over rules on \(\mathbb R^{\mathcal C_d}\) with Borel-measurable sections. Restricting any ambient rule to \(\mathsf{Prob}(\mathcal C_d)\) gives an admissible exact-side rule, so the exact-side class is at least as large after restriction, and therefore \[\mathfrak R_\star\le \widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star .\] Conversely, Lemma 28 gives \(\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\le\mathfrak R_m\) for every \(m\ge0\). For completeness, its measurable conditional-average construction in the present coordinates is as follows. Fix an integer \(m\ge0\) and an empirical-side rule \(\varphi:\mathcal Z_n\times\mathcal C_d^m\to[-1,1]\). Define first the polynomial conditional average on the ambient table space by \[\psi_\varphi^\circ(z,w) = \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m w(u_j)\Bigr)\varphi(z,u), \qquad w\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal C_d}.\] This function is Borel measurable in \(w\) for each fixed \(z\). Use its clipped version as the ambient exact-table rule, \[\psi_\varphi(z,w) = \min\{1,\max\{-1,\psi_\varphi^\circ(z,w)\}\}, \qquad w\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal C_d}.\] Then \(\psi_\varphi:\mathcal Z_n\times\mathbb R^{\mathcal C_d}\to[-1,1]\) is admissible for \(\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\). When \(w=r\in\mathsf{Prob}(\mathcal C_d)\), the coefficients \(\prod_{j=1}^m r(u_j)\) form a probability vector on \(\mathcal C_d^m\), and since \(\varphi\) takes values in \([-1,1]\), the polynomial average already lies in \([-1,1]\). Hence, on probability tables, \[\psi_\varphi(z,r) = \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m r(u_j)\Bigr)\varphi(z,u).\] For every \(\theta\) and \(z\), Jensen’s inequality for the product law \(\xi_\theta^{\otimes m}\) gives \[\bigl(\psi_\varphi(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 = \left( \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m\xi_\theta(u_j)\Bigr) \bigl(\varphi(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr) \right)^2 \le \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m\xi_\theta(u_j)\Bigr) \bigl(\varphi(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] Multiplying by \(\lambda_\theta(z)\), summing over \(z\), and then taking the supremum over \(\theta\) gives \[\sup_{\theta} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi_\varphi(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 \le \sup_{\theta} \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m\xi_\theta(u_j)\Bigr) \bigl(\varphi(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] Taking the infimum over \(\varphi\) yields \[\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\le \mathfrak R_m \qquad\text{for every }m\ge0 .\] Taking the limit along the displayed convergence yields \[\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\le \lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m=\mathfrak R_\star .\] The two inequalities identify the ambient measurable exact-table value with the exact-side value: \[\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star=\mathfrak R_\star .\]

  3. We use here the standing theorem hypotheses \(d\ge2\) and \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), which are also the parameter-range clauses in the definition of \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). Under these hypotheses, the compact coordinates parametrize the laws in \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) from Definition 6. For every \(\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}\), the law \(P_\theta\) satisfies occupied-cell overlap because, whenever \(\pi_x>0\), its treatment propensity is \[\frac{\sum_y P_\theta(x,1,y)}{\sum_{\alpha,y}P_\theta(x,\alpha,y)} =\varrho_x\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon].\] Conversely, given any \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), choose coordinates by \[\pi_x=p_x, \qquad \varrho_x= \begin{cases} e_x, & p_x>0,\\ \epsilon, & p_x=0, \end{cases} \qquad \omega_{\alpha x}=\mu_{\alpha x}.\] The value of \(\varrho_x\) in an empty cell is immaterial because every atom in that cell is multiplied by \(\pi_x=0\). For occupied cells, the overlap condition places \(\varrho_x\) in \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\), and the binary outcome regressions place \(\omega_{\alpha x}\) in \([0,1]\). These coordinates recover the atom probabilities, \[P(x,\alpha,y) = \pi_x\varrho_x^\alpha(1-\varrho_x)^{1-\alpha} \omega_{\alpha x}^y(1-\omega_{\alpha x})^{1-y},\] with the empty-cell case giving zero on both sides. Thus the map \(\theta\mapsto P_\theta\) is surjective onto the model class.

    Under this parametrization, the three quantities entering the known-marginal risk are transported by \[P_\theta^{\otimes n}(z)=\lambda_\theta(z), \qquad (P_\theta)_{XA}(x,\alpha)=\xi_\theta(x,\alpha), \qquad \tau(P_\theta)=\upsilon_\theta .\] The last identity follows from Definition 8: on occupied cells the outcome regressions are \(\omega_{1x}\) and \(\omega_{0x}\), while empty cells have coefficient \(\pi_x=0\). Hence, for every bounded measurable known-table rule \(\psi\), \[\int \bigl(\psi(Z,(P_\theta)_{XA})-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \,dP_\theta^{\otimes n}(Z) = \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .\] The known-table sample space in Definition 15 is \(\mathcal Z_n\times\mathbb R^{\mathcal C_d}\). Its bounded measurable procedures are in bijection with the ambient procedures used in \(\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star\). Explicitly, a paper procedure \(f\) is sent to \[\psi_f(z,w):=f(z,w),\] and an ambient procedure \(\psi\) is sent back to \(f_\psi(z,w):=\psi(z,w)\). Because \(\mathcal Z_n\) is finite and discrete, measurability of every section \(w\mapsto\psi(z,w)\) is equivalent to measurability of the uncurried map; the two assignments are pointwise inverse and preserve the range \([-1,1]\).

    For corresponding \(f\) and \(\psi_f\), evaluation at the known table \(w=(P_\theta)_{XA}=\xi_\theta\), followed by expansion of the finite labeled product law, gives \[\begin{aligned} &\int \bigl(f(Z,(P_\theta)_{XA})-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \,dP_\theta^{\otimes n}(Z)\\ &\qquad = \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \bigl(\psi_f(z,\xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2. \end{aligned}\] Thus the estimator bijection preserves every coordinate risk exactly. Reindexing the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) by the surjective coordinate map, and then taking the same infimum over the bijective procedure classes, gives \[R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d) = \widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star .\] Together with the equality \(\widetilde{\mathfrak R}_\star=\mathfrak R_\star\) above, this gives \[R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)=\mathfrak R_\star .\]

  4. For each \(m\), the minimax risk in Definition 11 equals the empirical finite-side value: \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)=\mathfrak R_m .\] First reindex the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) by the surjective map \(\theta\mapsto P_\theta\). The annotation sample space is the finite product \(\mathcal Z_n\times\mathcal C_d^m\), so every real-valued map on it is measurable. For any measurable estimator \(T\) in the annotation experiment, write its two-sample mean-squared risk at \(P_\theta\) as \[\mathcal V_T(\theta) = \int \bigl(T(Z,U)-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \,d\bigl(P_\theta^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_\theta)_{XA}^{\otimes m}\bigr)(Z,U).\] Expanding both finite product measures, and using \((P_\theta)_{XA}=\xi_\theta\), \(P_\theta^{\otimes n}=\lambda_\theta\), and \(\tau(P_\theta)=\upsilon_\theta\), gives the exact table identity \[\mathcal V_{T_\varphi}(\theta) = \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z_n}\lambda_\theta(z) \sum_{u\in\mathcal C_d^m} \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m\xi_\theta(u_j)\Bigr) \bigl(\varphi(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2\] for \(T_\varphi(z,u):=\varphi(z,u)\).

    Thus every empirical-side procedure \(\varphi:\mathcal Z_n\times\mathcal C_d^m\to[-1,1]\) induces the paper estimator \[T_\varphi(z,u)=\varphi(z,u),\] viewed as an estimator in the annotation experiment. For this induced estimator, the preceding expansion gives equality of the coordinate risks for every \(\theta\), hence equality of the corresponding worst-case risks after the reindexing by \(\theta\). Since the paper infimum ranges over all measurable estimators, including these induced estimators, taking the infimum over \(\varphi\) gives \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\le \mathfrak R_m .\] For the reverse inequality, let \(T\) be any measurable paper estimator and define the clipped empirical-side rule \[\varphi_T(z,u) = \min\{1,\max\{-1,T(z,u)\}\}.\] The transported identity \(\tau(P_\theta)=\upsilon_\theta\), together with the target-range bound, gives the pointwise clipping inequality \[\bigl(\varphi_T(z,u)-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \le \bigl(T(z,u)-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \qquad\text{for all }(z,u),\theta .\] Integrating this inequality against \(P_\theta^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_\theta)_{XA}^{\otimes m}\) yields \[\int \bigl(\varphi_T(Z,U)-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \,d\bigl(P_\theta^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_\theta)_{XA}^{\otimes m}\bigr)(Z,U) \le \mathcal V_T(\theta).\] By the same finite product expansion as above, the left side is exactly the empirical-side table risk of \(\varphi_T\) at \(\theta\). The comparison of worst-case risks also requires a finite bound for the unclipped estimator. Set \[B_T:=\sum_{(z,u)\in\mathcal Z_n\times\mathcal C_d^m} \bigl(|T(z,u)|+1\bigr)^2 .\] Since \(|\upsilon_\theta|\le1\), \[\bigl(T(z,u)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 \le \bigl(|T(z,u)|+1\bigr)^2 \le B_T\] for every \((z,u)\) and \(\theta\). Hence the worst-case risk of \(T\) over the reindexed model is bounded, and the integrated clipping inequality implies \[\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}} \int \bigl(\varphi_T(Z,U)-\tau(P_\theta)\bigr)^2 \,d\bigl(P_\theta^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_\theta)_{XA}^{\otimes m}\bigr)(Z,U) \le \sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\epsilon,d}}\mathcal V_T(\theta).\] Taking infima over all measurable paper estimators \(T\) gives \[\mathfrak R_m\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] The two inequalities prove \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)=\mathfrak R_m\).

  5. The preceding displayed identities give \[\lim_{m\to\infty}R_\epsilon(n,m,d) = \lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak R_m = \mathfrak R_\star = R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d).\]

∎
Proof of Theorem 6.

  1. By Lemma 2, choose \(C_0>0\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that for every \(d\), every \(u,t>0\), and every \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), \[\operatorname{Var}_P(Z^H)\le C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1}), \qquad \bigl|E_P Z^H-\tau(P)\bigr|\le C_0\min\{1,d/t\}.\] Hence, in the independent Poisson count experiment, \[E_P\{Z^H-\tau(P)\}^2 = \operatorname{Var}_P(Z^H)+\{E_PZ^H-\tau(P)\}^2 \le C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1}) + C_0^2\min\{1,d/t\}^2 .\] Set \[C_\epsilon=100000(1+C_0+C_0^2)>0 .\]

  2. Fix \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\). Let \[M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad n_p=\left\lfloor\frac{n-M_0}{2}\right\rfloor,\qquad n_f=n-M_0-n_p,\qquad m_f=m-\lfloor m/2\rfloor ,\] and put \[u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad t=\frac{n_f+m_f}{8},\qquad \mathfrak q=\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2}.\] The estimator is a finite sum of measurable prefix statistics with deterministic weights, so it is measurable. The class \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) is nonempty: the law with uniform covariate distribution, propensity \(1/2\), and equal outcome regressions in the two arms satisfies Assumption 1 because \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).

  3. First suppose \(24\le n\). The block arithmetic gives \[n\le4M_0,\qquad n+m\le4(n_f+m_f),\] and therefore \(M_0>0\), \(n_f+m_f>0\), \(u>0\), and \(t>0\). Consequently \[\frac n{32}\le u,\qquad \frac{n+m}{32}\le t,\] so \[u^{-1}\le\frac{32}{n}, \qquad t^{-1}\le\frac{32}{n+m}\le\frac{32}{n}.\] Also, \[\frac dt\le \frac{32d}{n+m},\] and hence \[\min\{1,d/t\}^2 \le (d/t)^2 \le 1024\,\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2}.\]

  4. For the Poisson prefix overflow terms, the elementary bound \[\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/8)>\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}\} \le \frac8{\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad \Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}\ge1,\] is obtained as follows. Bernstein’s upper-tail inequality for a Poisson variable with mean \(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/8\), applied with deviation parameter \(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/4\), yields \[\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/8)>\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}\} \le \exp(-\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/4).\] Since \(\exp(x)\ge 1+x\), \[\exp(-\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/4)=\frac1{\exp(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/4)} \le \frac1{\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}/4} =\frac4{\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}} \le\frac8{\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}} .\] Applying this with \(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}=M_0\) and \(\Lambda_{\mathrm{tail}}=n_f+m_f\) gives \[\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\}\le \frac8{M_0}, \qquad \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>n_f+m_f\}\le \frac8{n_f+m_f}.\] Together with \(n\le4M_0\) and \(n+m\le4(n_f+m_f)\), this implies \[\frac8{M_0}\le \frac{32}{n}, \qquad \frac8{n_f+m_f}\le \frac{32}{n+m}\le \frac{32}{n}.\]

  5. Fix \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). Under the probability law \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}\), draw two independent finite prefixes: an outcome-marked prefix from \(P\) with Poisson length of mean \(u\), and a treatment–covariate prefix from \(P_{XA}\) with Poisson length of mean \(t\). If \(S_{ax}\) is the number of outcome-marked prefix records with \((X,A,Y)=(x,a,1)\) and \(K_{ax}\) is the number of treatment–covariate prefix records with \((X,A)=(x,a)\), set \[\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,pref}}_{u,t} = \operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]} \left[ \sum_{x=1}^d \left\{ \frac{S_{1x}}u\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{1x}+1} - \frac{S_{0x}}u\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{0x}+1} \right\} \right],\] and define \[\mathcal R_{\mathrm{pref}}(P;u,t) := E_{\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}} \left[ \left\{ \widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,pref}}_{u,t}-\tau(P) \right\}^2 \right].\] The prefix transfer in Lemma 4, applied with the present block sizes and intensities, gives \[E_P\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le \mathcal R_{\mathrm{pref}}(P;u,t) + 4\left[ \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\} + \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>n_f+m_f\} \right].\] Here the fixed-sample estimator is the finite Rao–Blackwell average over admissible prefix lengths in Definition 20; the displayed inequality accounts for the two overflow events, and the coefficient \(4\) is the squared diameter of \([-1,1]\) for the clipped statistic and the target \(\tau(P)\).

  6. The Poisson-prefix counts have the Poisson count law by Lemma 3: for every \(x\in[d]\) and \(a\in\{0,1\}\), \[S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}), \qquad K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),\] and these coordinates are mutually independent. Thus the pushforward of \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}\) under the count map is exactly the product law used in Lemma 2. We first record the range of the target. The cell masses satisfy \(p_x\ge0\) and \(\sum_{x=1}^d p_x=1\), and the binary-outcome regressions satisfy \(0\le\mu_{ax}\le1\). Hence \[\left|\tau(P)\right| = \left|\sum_{x=1}^d p_x(\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x})\right| \le \sum_{x=1}^d p_x\,|\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x}| \le \sum_{x=1}^d p_x =1 .\] Thus \(\tau(P)\in[-1,1]\). Since projection onto \([-1,1]\) cannot increase distance from a point of \([-1,1]\), clipping gives \[\mathcal R_{\mathrm{pref}}(P;u,t) \le E_P\{Z^H-\tau(P)\}^2 .\] Combining this with Step 1 gives \[\mathcal R_{\mathrm{pref}}(P;u,t) \le C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1}) + C_0^2\min\{1,d/t\}^2 .\]

  7. The preceding displays imply \[E_P\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le \left(64C_0+1024C_0^2+256\right) \left\{ \frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2} \right\}.\] Indeed, the variance contribution is bounded by \[C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1})\le 64C_0\,\frac1n,\] the squared-bias contribution by \[C_0^2\min\{1,d/t\}^2 \le 1024C_0^2\,\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2},\] and the overflow contribution by \[4\left(\frac8{M_0}+\frac8{n_f+m_f}\right) \le 256\,\frac1n .\] Since \[64C_0+1024C_0^2+256\le C_\epsilon,\] the preceding display gives the large-sample estimate \[E_P\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le C_\epsilon\mathfrak q .\] If \(\mathfrak q\le1\), then \(\min\{1,\mathfrak q\}=\mathfrak q\), and hence \[E_P\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right] \le C_\epsilon\min\{1,\mathfrak q\}.\] When \(\mathfrak q>1\), both the estimator and \(\tau(P)\) lie in \([-1,1]\), so \[E_P\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right]\le4 \le C_\epsilon\min\{1,\mathfrak q\}.\]

  8. It remains to cover \(n<24\). Then \(n\le23\), and \[\frac1{23}\le \frac1n\le \mathfrak q, \qquad \frac1{23}\le \min\{1,\mathfrak q\}.\] Again using the boundedness of the estimator and of \(\tau(P)\), \[E_P\!\left[ \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2 \right]\le4 \le C_\epsilon\min\{1,\mathfrak q\},\] because \(C_\epsilon\ge92\).

  9. Taking the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) in the preceding bound gives \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}} E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}} \left[ \left\{ \widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P) \right\}^2 \right] \le C_\epsilon \min\left\{ 1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2} \right\}.\] Finally, \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}\) is an admissible measurable estimator in the class defining \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) in Definition 11. Therefore \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \le C_\epsilon \min\left\{ 1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2} \right\}.\]

∎
Proof of Theorem 7.

Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).

  1. By Lemma 22, choose \(c_{\mathrm{lab}}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), \[\frac{c_{\mathrm{lab}}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).\] By Theorem 6, choose \(C_{\mathrm{IC}}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), \[R_\epsilon(n,m,d) \le C_{\mathrm{IC}} \min\left\{ 1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2} \right\}.\] Set \[c_\epsilon:=c_{\mathrm{lab}}, \qquad C_\epsilon:=\max\{c_{\mathrm{lab}},5C_{\mathrm{IC}}\}.\] Then \(0<c_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon\).

  2. For \(d=2\), the lower bound supplied by Lemma 22 gives, for every \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\), \[\frac{c_\epsilon}{n} = \frac{c_{\mathrm{lab}}}{n} \le R_\epsilon(n,m,2).\]

  3. It remains to bound the right-hand side. Fix \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\), and put \[N:=n+m .\] Then \(N\ge n\ge1\), hence \(N>0\), and \[n\le N\le N^2,\] because \(N\ge1\). Therefore \[\frac{4}{N^2}\le \frac4n .\] Using Theorem 6 with \(d=2\), \[\begin{aligned} R_\epsilon(n,m,2) &\le C_{\mathrm{IC}} \min\left\{ 1,\frac1n+\frac{4}{N^2} \right\} \\ &\le C_{\mathrm{IC}} \left(\frac1n+\frac{4}{N^2}\right) \\ &\le C_{\mathrm{IC}}\frac5n = \frac{5C_{\mathrm{IC}}}{n} \le \frac{C_\epsilon}{n}. \end{aligned}\] This proves the asserted two-sided bound for all \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\).

∎
